					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom from 1916 to 1922</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Lloyd George" redirects here. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Lloyd_George_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Lloyd George (disambiguation)">Lloyd George (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This <a href="/wiki/Double-barrelled_name#British_tradition" title="Double-barrelled name">British surname is barrelled</a>, being made up of multiple names. It should be written as <i>Lloyd George</i>, not <i>George</i>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

.navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Liberalism_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Template:Liberalism in the United Kingdom"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Liberalism_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Template talk:Liberalism in the United Kingdom"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Liberalism_in_the_United_Kingdom&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>David Lloyd George, 1st Earl Lloyd-George of Dwyfor</b>, <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Member_of_the_Order_of_Merit" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of the Order of Merit">OM</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Knight_of_Justice_of_the_Order_of_St_John" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of Justice of the Order of St John">KStJ</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">PC</a></span> (17 January 1863 – 26 March 1945) was <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> from 1916 to 1922. A <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(United_Kingdom)" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party (United Kingdom)">Liberal Party</a> politician from Wales, he was known for leading the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland" title="United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland">United Kingdom</a> during the <a href="/wiki/First_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First World War">First World War</a>, for social-reform policies (including the <a href="/wiki/National_Insurance_Act_1911" title="National Insurance Act 1911">National Insurance Act 1911</a>), for his role in the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference_(1919%E2%80%931920)" title="Paris Peace Conference (1919–1920)">Paris Peace Conference</a>, and for negotiating the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Free_State" title="Irish Free State">Irish Free State</a>. He was the last Liberal Party prime minister; the party fell into third-party status shortly after the end of his premiership.
</p><p>After becoming active in local politics, Lloyd George gained a reputation as an orator and a proponent of a Welsh blend of radical Liberal ideas, which included support for <a href="/wiki/Welsh_devolution" title="Welsh devolution">Welsh devolution</a>, for the <a href="/wiki/Disestablishment_of_the_Church_in_Wales" class="mw-redirect" title="Disestablishment of the Church in Wales">disestablishment</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> <a href="/wiki/Church_in_Wales" title="Church in Wales">in Wales</a>, for equality for labourers and tenant farmers, and for reform of land ownership. In 1890 he narrowly won <a href="/wiki/1890_Caernarvon_Boroughs_by-election" title="1890 Caernarvon Boroughs by-election">a by-election</a> to become the Member of Parliament for <a href="/wiki/Caernarvon_Boroughs" class="mw-redirect" title="Caernarvon Boroughs">Caernarvon Boroughs</a>, in which seat he remained for 55 years. He served in <a href="/wiki/Henry_Campbell-Bannerman" title="Henry Campbell-Bannerman">Henry Campbell-Bannerman</a>'s cabinet from 1905. After <a href="/wiki/H._H._Asquith" title="H. H. Asquith">H. H. Asquith</a> succeeded to the premiership in 1908, Lloyd George replaced him as <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">Chancellor of the Exchequer</a>. To fund extensive <a href="/wiki/Liberal_welfare_reforms" title="Liberal welfare reforms">welfare reforms</a> he proposed taxes on land ownership and high incomes in the "<a href="/wiki/People%27s_Budget" title="People&#39;s Budget">People's Budget</a>" (1909), which the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a>-dominated <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a> rejected. The resulting <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_crisis" title="Constitutional crisis">constitutional crisis</a> was only resolved after two elections in 1910 and the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Act_1911" title="Parliament Act 1911">1911 Parliament Act</a>. His budget was enacted in 1910, and the National Insurance Act 1911 and other measures helped to establish the modern <a href="/wiki/Welfare_state" title="Welfare state">welfare state</a>. In 1913, he was embroiled in the <a href="/wiki/Marconi_scandal" title="Marconi scandal">Marconi scandal</a>, but he remained in office and promoted the disestablishment of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> in Wales until 1914, when its implementation was suspended in response to the outbreak of the First World War.
</p><p>As wartime chancellor, Lloyd George strengthened the country's finances and forged agreements with trade unions to maintain production. In 1915, Asquith formed a <a href="/wiki/Asquith_coalition_ministry" title="Asquith coalition ministry">Liberal-led wartime coalition</a> with the Conservatives and <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour</a>. Lloyd George became <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Munitions" title="Minister of Munitions">Minister of Munitions</a> and rapidly expanded production. Amongst other measures, he set up four large munitions factories as a countermeasure to the <a href="/wiki/Shell_Crisis_of_1915" title="Shell Crisis of 1915">shell crisis</a> of the previous year. The so-called '<a href="/wiki/National_Filling_Factory,_Georgetown" title="National Filling Factory, Georgetown">National Filling Factory</a>' in <a href="/wiki/Renfrewshire_(historic)" title="Renfrewshire (historic)">Renfrewshire</a> was named 'Georgetown' in Lloyd George's honour.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> In 1916, he was appointed <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_War" title="Secretary of State for War">Secretary of State for War</a> but was frustrated by his limited power and by clashes with the military establishment over strategy. Amid stalemate on the <a href="/wiki/Western_Front_(World_War_I)" title="Western Front (World War I)">Western Front</a>, confidence in Asquith's leadership as prime minister waned, and he resigned in December 1916. Lloyd George succeeded him as prime minister, supported by the Conservatives and some Liberals. He centralised authority by creating a smaller <a href="/wiki/War_cabinet" title="War cabinet">war cabinet</a>, a new <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_Office" title="Cabinet Office">Cabinet Office</a> and what he called his "<a href="/wiki/The_Garden_Suburb" title="The Garden Suburb">Garden Suburb</a>" of advisers. To combat food shortages he implemented the convoy system, established rationing, and stimulated farming. After supporting the disastrous French <a href="/wiki/Nivelle_Offensive" class="mw-redirect" title="Nivelle Offensive">Nivelle Offensive</a> in 1917, he had to reluctantly approve <a href="/wiki/Douglas_Haig,_1st_Earl_Haig" title="Douglas Haig, 1st Earl Haig">Field Marshal Haig</a>'s plans for the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Passchendaele" title="Battle of Passchendaele">Battle of Passchendaele</a>, which again resulted in huge casualties with little strategic benefit. Against the views of British military commanders, he was finally able to see the Allies brought under one command in March 1918. The war effort turned in their favour in August and was won in November. In the aftermath, and following the December 1918 <a href="/wiki/1918_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1918 United Kingdom general election">"Coupon" election</a>, he and the Conservatives maintained their coalition with popular support. Earlier that year his government <a href="/wiki/Representation_of_the_People_Act_1918" title="Representation of the People Act 1918">had extended</a> <a href="/wiki/Suffrage" title="Suffrage">the franchise</a> to all men and some women.
</p><p>Lloyd George was a major player in the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference,_1919" class="mw-redirect" title="Paris Peace Conference, 1919">Paris Peace Conference</a> of 1919, but the situation in Ireland worsened that year, erupting into the <a href="/wiki/Irish_War_of_Independence" title="Irish War of Independence">Irish War of Independence</a>, which lasted until Lloyd George negotiated independence from the UK for the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Free_State" title="Irish Free State">Irish Free State</a> in 1921. At home, he initiated reforms to education and housing, but trade-union militancy rose to record levels, the economy <a href="/wiki/Depression_of_1920%E2%80%9321" class="mw-redirect" title="Depression of 1920–21">became depressed in 1920</a> and unemployment rose; <a href="/wiki/Geddes_Axe" title="Geddes Axe">spending cuts</a> followed in 1921–22, and in 1922 he became embroiled in a scandal over the sale of honours and the <a href="/wiki/Chanak_Crisis" title="Chanak Crisis">Chanak Crisis</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Carlton_Club_meeting" title="Carlton Club meeting">Carlton Club meeting</a> resulted in backbench support for the Conservatives to end the coalition and to contest the next election alone. Lloyd George resigned as prime minister and never held office again, but continued as the leader of a Liberal faction. After an awkward reunion with Asquith's faction in 1923, Lloyd George led the Liberals from 1926 to 1931. He put forward innovative proposals for public works and other reforms in a series of coloured books, but made only modest gains in the <a href="/wiki/1929_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1929 United Kingdom general election">1929 election</a>. After 1931, he was a mistrusted figure heading a small rump of breakaway Liberals who were opposed to the <a href="/wiki/National_Government_(United_Kingdom)" title="National Government (United Kingdom)">National Government</a>. In 1940, he refused to serve in <a href="/wiki/Churchill_war_ministry" title="Churchill war ministry">Winston Churchill's War Cabinet</a>. He was elevated to the peerage in 1945, shortly before his death.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>David George was born on 17 January 1863 in <a href="/wiki/Chorlton-on-Medlock" title="Chorlton-on-Medlock">Chorlton-on-Medlock</a>, Manchester, to <a href="/wiki/Welsh_people" title="Welsh people">Welsh</a> parents William George and Elizabeth Lloyd George. William George had previously been married to Selina Huntley, who died in 1855 aged 36. William and Elizabeth's first child was a son, David, born in September 1860, who only lived 12 hours.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>William George had been a teacher in both London and Liverpool. He also taught in the <a href="/wiki/General_Assembly_of_Unitarian_and_Free_Christian_Churches" title="General Assembly of Unitarian and Free Christian Churches">Unitarian</a>-administered Hope Street Sunday Schools, where he met Unitarian minister <a href="/wiki/James_Martineau" title="James Martineau">James Martineau</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyEducation_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyEducation-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1">&#58;&#8202;1&#8202;</span></sup> In March 1863, on account of his failing health, William George returned with his family to his native <a href="/wiki/Pembrokeshire" title="Pembrokeshire">Pembrokeshire</a>. He took up farming but died in June 1864 of <a href="/wiki/Pneumonia" title="Pneumonia">pneumonia</a>, aged 44. David was just over one year old.
</p><p>His widow, Elizabeth George (1828–96), sold the farm and moved with her children to her native <a href="/wiki/Llanystumdwy" title="Llanystumdwy">Llanystumdwy</a> in Caernarfonshire, where she lived in a cottage known as Highgate with her brother Richard. Richard Lloyd (1834–1917) was a shoemaker, a <a href="/wiki/Minister_(Christianity)" title="Minister (Christianity)">minister</a> (first in the <a href="/wiki/Baptist_Union_of_Scotland" title="Baptist Union of Scotland">Scottish Baptists</a> and then in the <a href="/wiki/Restoration_Movement#Great_Britain" title="Restoration Movement">Church of Christ</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> and a strong Liberal. Richard Lloyd was a towering influence on his nephew until his death in 1917 and was the first to encourage his nephew to take up a career in law and enter politics. David adopted his uncle's surname to become "Lloyd George".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="The time period mentioned near this tag is ambiguous. (December 2022)">when?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Lloyd George was educated at the local <a href="/wiki/Anglican" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican">Anglican</a> school, Llanystumdwy <a href="/wiki/National_school_(England_and_Wales)" title="National school (England and Wales)">National School</a>, and later under tutors.
</p><p>He was brought up with <a href="/wiki/Welsh_language" title="Welsh language">Welsh</a> as his first language;<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyEducation_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyEducation-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Roy_Jenkins" title="Roy Jenkins">Roy Jenkins</a>, another Welsh politician, notes that, "Lloyd George was Welsh, that his whole culture, his whole outlook, his language was Welsh."<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Though Lloyd George cited the influence of his childhood throughout his career, biographer <a href="/wiki/John_Grigg_(writer)" class="mw-redirect" title="John Grigg (writer)">John Grigg</a> argues that his childhood was nowhere near as poverty-stricken as he liked to suggest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrigg1997Preface_to_New_Paperback_Edition,_p._12_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrigg1997Preface_to_New_Paperback_Edition,_p._12-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though brought up a devout evangelical, Lloyd George privately lost his religious faith as a young man. Biographer Don Cregier says he became "a Deist and perhaps an agnostic, though he remained a chapel-goer and connoisseur of good preaching all his life." He was nevertheless, according to <a href="/wiki/Frank_Owen_(politician)" title="Frank Owen (politician)">Frank Owen</a>, "one of the foremost fighting leaders of a fanatical Welsh <a href="/wiki/Nonconformist_(Protestantism)" title="Nonconformist (Protestantism)">Nonconformity</a>" for a quarter of a century.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyEducation_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyEducation-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 6">&#58;&#8202;6&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Cregier_1976_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cregier_1976-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwen195431_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwen195431-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Legal_practice_and_early_politics">Legal practice and early politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Legal practice and early politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Lloyd_George_NLW3362532_cropped.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/David_Lloyd_George_NLW3362532_cropped.jpg/170px-David_Lloyd_George_NLW3362532_cropped.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="276" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/David_Lloyd_George_NLW3362532_cropped.jpg/255px-David_Lloyd_George_NLW3362532_cropped.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/7f/David_Lloyd_George_NLW3362532_cropped.jpg 2x" data-file-width="270" data-file-height="439" /></a><figcaption>Lloyd George <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1890</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Lloyd George qualified as a solicitor in 1884 after being <a href="/wiki/Articled_clerk" title="Articled clerk">articled</a> to a firm in <a href="/wiki/Porthmadog" title="Porthmadog">Porthmadog</a> and taking Honours in his final law examination. He set up his own practice in the back parlour of his uncle's house in 1885.<sup id="cite_ref-Bourns2016_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bourns2016-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Although many prime ministers have been <a href="/wiki/Barrister" title="Barrister">barristers</a>, Lloyd George is, as of 2023, the only solicitor to have held that office.<sup id="cite_ref-Bourns2016_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bourns2016-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a solicitor, Lloyd George was politically active from the start, campaigning for his uncle's <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal Party</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1885_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1885 United Kingdom general election">1885 election</a>. He was attracted by <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Chamberlain" title="Joseph Chamberlain">Joseph Chamberlain</a>'s "unauthorised programme" of <a href="/wiki/Radicals_(UK)" title="Radicals (UK)">Radical</a> reform.<sup id="cite_ref-RowlandBackParlour_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RowlandBackParlour-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 43">&#58;&#8202;43&#8202;</span></sup> After the election, Chamberlain split with Gladstone in opposition to Irish <a href="/wiki/Home_Rule" class="mw-redirect" title="Home Rule">Home Rule</a>, and Lloyd George moved to join the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Unionists" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Unionists">Liberal Unionists</a>. Uncertain of which wing to follow, he moved a resolution in support of Chamberlain at a local Liberal club and travelled to <a href="/wiki/Birmingham" title="Birmingham">Birmingham</a> to attend the first meeting of Chamberlain's new National Radical Union, but arrived a week too early.<sup id="cite_ref-RowlandBackParlour_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RowlandBackParlour-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 53">&#58;&#8202;53&#8202;</span></sup> In 1907 Lloyd George would tell <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Lewis" title="Herbert Lewis">Herbert Lewis</a> that he had thought Chamberlain's plan for a federal solution to the Home Rule Question correct in 1886 and still thought so, and that "If Henry Richmond, Osborne Morgan and the Welsh members had stood by Chamberlain on an agreement as regards the [Welsh] disestablishment, they would have carried Wales with them"<sup id="cite_ref-RowlandBackParlour_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RowlandBackParlour-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 53">&#58;&#8202;53&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>His legal practice quickly flourished; he established branch offices in surrounding towns and took his brother <a href="/wiki/William_George_(solicitor)" title="William George (solicitor)">William</a> into partnership in 1887.<sup id="cite_ref-Bourns2016_11-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bourns2016-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Lloyd George's legal and political triumph came in the <a href="/wiki/Llanfrothen" title="Llanfrothen">Llanfrothen</a> burial case, which established the right of <a href="/wiki/Nonconformist_(Protestantism)" title="Nonconformist (Protestantism)">Nonconformists</a> to be buried according to denominational rites in parish burial grounds, as given by the <a href="/wiki/Burial_Laws_Amendment_Act_1880" title="Burial Laws Amendment Act 1880">Burial Laws Amendment Act 1880</a> but theretofore ignored by the <a href="/wiki/Anglican" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican">Anglican</a> clergy. On Lloyd George's advice, a Baptist burial party broke open a gate to a cemetery that had been locked against them by the vicar. The vicar sued them for trespass and although the jury returned a verdict for the party, the local judge misrecorded the jury's verdict and found in the vicar's favour. Suspecting bias, Lloyd George's clients won on appeal to the Divisional Court of Queen's Bench in London, where Lord Chief Justice <a href="/wiki/John_Coleridge,_1st_Baron_Coleridge" title="John Coleridge, 1st Baron Coleridge">Coleridge</a> found in their favour.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyEducation_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyEducation-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 14–15">&#58;&#8202;14–15&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> The case was hailed as a great victory throughout Wales and led to Lloyd George's adoption as the Liberal candidate for <a href="/wiki/Caernarfon_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Caernarfon (UK Parliament constituency)">Carnarvon Boroughs</a> on 27 December 1888.<sup id="cite_ref-HatterNotAGent_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HatterNotAGent-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 46">&#58;&#8202;46&#8202;</span></sup> The same year, he and other young Welsh Liberals founded a monthly paper, <i>Udgorn Rhyddid</i> (Bugle of Freedom).<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyEducation_5-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyEducation-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 14–15">&#58;&#8202;14–15&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>In 1889, Lloyd George became an <a href="/wiki/Alderman" title="Alderman">alderman</a> on <a href="/wiki/Carnarvonshire" class="mw-redirect" title="Carnarvonshire">Carnarvonshire</a> <a href="/wiki/County_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="County Council">County Council</a> (a new body which had been created by the <a href="/wiki/Local_Government_Act_1888" title="Local Government Act 1888">Local Government Act 1888</a>) and would remain so for the rest of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyEducation_5-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyEducation-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 15">&#58;&#8202;15&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-RowlandBackParlour_12-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RowlandBackParlour-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 65–66">&#58;&#8202;65–66&#8202;</span></sup> Lloyd George would also serve the county as a <a href="/wiki/Justice_of_the_Peace" class="mw-redirect" title="Justice of the Peace">Justice of the Peace</a> (1910), chairman of <a href="/wiki/Quarter_Sessions" class="mw-redirect" title="Quarter Sessions">Quarter Sessions</a> (1929–38), and <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Lieutenant" class="mw-redirect" title="Deputy Lieutenant">Deputy Lieutenant</a> in 1921.<sup id="cite_ref-kellys_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kellys-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-bpeerage_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bpeerage-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriage">Marriage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Marriage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Lloyd George married <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Lloyd_George" title="Margaret Lloyd George">Margaret Owen</a>, the daughter of a well-to-do local farming family, on 24 January 1888.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyEducation_5-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyEducation-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 15–16">&#58;&#8202;15–16&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_years_as_a_member_of_Parliament_.281890.E2.80.931905.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years_as_a_member_of_Parliament_(1890–1905)">Early years as a member of Parliament (1890–1905)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Early years as a member of Parliament (1890–1905)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Lloyd George's career as a member of parliament began when he was returned as a Liberal MP for <a href="/wiki/Caernarfon_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Caernarfon (UK Parliament constituency)">Caernarfon Boroughs</a> (now <a href="/wiki/Caernarfon_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Caernarfon (UK Parliament constituency)">Caernarfon</a>), narrowly winning the <a href="/wiki/1890_Caernarvon_Boroughs_by-election" title="1890 Caernarvon Boroughs by-election">by-election on 10 April 1890</a>, following the death of the Conservative member <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Swetenham" title="Edmund Swetenham">Edmund Swetenham</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGilbert1987ch._Childhood,_Youth,_the_Law_and_Politics,_pp._75–76_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilbert1987ch._Childhood,_Youth,_the_Law_and_Politics,_pp._75–76-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He would remain an MP for the same constituency until 1945, 55 years later.<sup id="cite_ref-HatterNotAGent_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HatterNotAGent-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Lloyd George's early beginnings in Westminster may have proven difficult for him as a radical liberal and "a great outsider".<sup id="cite_ref-HatterNotAGent_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HatterNotAGent-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Backbench members of the House of Commons were not paid at that time, so Lloyd George supported himself and his growing family by continuing to practise as a solicitor. He opened an office in London under the name of "Lloyd George and Co." and continued in partnership with William George in <a href="/wiki/Criccieth" title="Criccieth">Criccieth</a>. In 1897, he merged his growing London practice with that of Arthur Rhys Roberts (who was to become <a href="/wiki/Official_Solicitor" title="Official Solicitor">Official Solicitor</a>) under the name of "Lloyd George, Roberts and Co."<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Welsh_affairs">Welsh affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Welsh affairs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Kenneth_O._Morgan" title="Kenneth O. Morgan">Kenneth O. Morgan</a> describes Lloyd George as a "lifelong Welsh nationalist" and suggests that between 1880 and 1914 he was "the symbol and tribune of the <a href="/wiki/Welsh_nationalism" title="Welsh nationalism">national reawakening of Wales</a>", although he is also clear that from the early 1900s his main focus gradually shifted to UK-wide issues.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-open.edu_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-open.edu-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He also became an associate of <a href="/wiki/T._E._Ellis" title="T. E. Ellis">Tom Ellis</a>, MP for Meirionydd, having previously told a Caernarfon friend in 1888 that he was a "Welsh Nationalist of the Ellis type".<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Decentralisation_and_Welsh_disestablishment">Decentralisation and Welsh disestablishment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Decentralisation and Welsh disestablishment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>One of Lloyd George's first acts as an MP was to organise an informal grouping of Welsh Liberal members with a programme that included; disestablishing and disendowing the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> in Wales, <a href="/wiki/Temperance_movement_in_Wales" class="mw-redirect" title="Temperance movement in Wales">temperance</a> reform, and establishing <a href="/wiki/Welsh_home_rule" class="mw-redirect" title="Welsh home rule">Welsh home rule</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-HatterNotAGent_14-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HatterNotAGent-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 50">&#58;&#8202;50&#8202;</span></sup> He was keen on <a href="/wiki/Decentralization" title="Decentralization">decentralisation</a> and thus <a href="/wiki/Welsh_devolution" title="Welsh devolution">Welsh devolution</a>, starting with the devolution of the <a href="/wiki/Church_in_Wales" title="Church in Wales">Church in Wales</a> saying in 1890: "I am deeply impressed with the fact that Wales has wants and inspirations of her own which have too long been ignored, but which must no longer be neglected. First and foremost amongst these stands the cause of Religious Liberty and Equality in Wales. If returned to Parliament by you, it shall be my earnest endeavour to labour for the triumph of this great cause. I believe in a liberal extension of the principle of Decentralization."<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the next decade, Lloyd George campaigned in Parliament largely on Welsh issues, in particular for disestablishment and disendowment of the Church of England. When Gladstone retired in 1894 after the defeat of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Home_Rule_Bill" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Home Rule Bill">second Home Rule Bill</a>, the Welsh Liberal members chose him to serve on a deputation to <a href="/wiki/William_Vernon_Harcourt_(politician)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Vernon Harcourt (politician)">William Harcourt</a> to press for specific assurances on Welsh issues. When those assurances were not provided, they resolved to take independent action if the government did not bring a bill for disestablishment. When a bill was not forthcoming, he and three other Welsh Liberals (<a href="/wiki/D._A._Thomas" title="D. A. Thomas">D. A. Thomas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Lewis" title="Herbert Lewis">Herbert Lewis</a> and <a href="/wiki/Frank_Edwards_(British_politician)" class="mw-redirect" title="Frank Edwards (British politician)">Frank Edwards</a>) refused the <a href="/wiki/Whip_(politics)" title="Whip (politics)">whip</a> on 14 April 1894, but accepted <a href="/wiki/Archibald_Primrose,_5th_Earl_of_Rosebery" title="Archibald Primrose, 5th Earl of Rosebery">Lord Rosebery</a>'s assurance and rejoined the official Liberals on 29 May.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Cymru_Fydd_and_Welsh_devolution"><span title="Welsh-language text"><span lang="cy" style="font-style: normal;">Cymru Fydd</span></span> and Welsh devolution</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Cymru Fydd and Welsh devolution">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Historian Emyr Price referred to Lloyd George as "the first architect of Welsh devolution and its most famous advocate" as well as "the pioneering advocate of a powerful parliament for the Welsh people".<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Lloyd George himself stated in 1880 "Is it not high time that Wales should the powers to manage its own affairs" and in 1890, "Parliament is so overweighted that it cannot possibly devote the time and trouble necessary to legislate for the peculiar and domestic retirement of each and every separate province of Britain". These statements would later be used to advocate for a Welsh assembly in the <a href="/wiki/1979_Welsh_devolution_referendum" title="1979 Welsh devolution referendum">1979 Welsh devolution referendum</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Lloyd George felt that disestablishment, land reform and other forms of Welsh devolution could only be achieved if Wales formed its own government within a federal imperial system.<sup id="cite_ref-open.edu_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-open.edu-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In 1895, in a failed Church in Wales Bill, Lloyd George added an amendment in a discreet attempt at forming a sort of Welsh home rule, a national council for appointment of the Welsh Church commissioners. Although not condemned by <a href="/wiki/T._E._Ellis" title="T. E. Ellis">Tom Ellis</a> MP, this was to the annoyance of <a href="/wiki/J._Bryn_Roberts" class="mw-redirect" title="J. Bryn Roberts">J. Bryn Roberts</a> MP and the Home Secretary <a href="/wiki/H._H._Asquith" title="H. H. Asquith">H. H. Asquith</a> MP.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was also a co-leader of <span title="Welsh-language text"><span lang="cy" style="font-style: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Cymru_Fydd" title="Cymru Fydd">Cymru Fydd</a></span></span>, a national Welsh party with liberal values with the goals of promoting a "stronger Welsh identity" and establishing a Welsh government.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> He hoped that <span title="Welsh-language text"><span lang="cy" style="font-style: normal;">Cymru Fydd</span></span> would become a force like the <a href="/wiki/Irish_National_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish National Party">Irish National Party</a>. He abandoned this idea after being criticised in Welsh newspapers for bringing about the defeat of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal Party</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1895_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1895 United Kingdom general election">1895 election</a>. In an AGM meeting in <a href="/wiki/Newport,_Wales" title="Newport, Wales">Newport</a> on 16 January 1896 of the South Wales Liberal Federation, led by <a href="/wiki/D._A._Thomas" title="D. A. Thomas">D. A. Thomas</a>, a proposal was made to unite the North and South Liberal Federations with <span title="Welsh-language text"><span lang="cy" style="font-style: normal;">Cymru Fydd</span></span> to form The Welsh National Federation. This was a proposal which the North Wales Liberal Federation had already agreed to. However, the South Wales Liberal Federation rejected this. According to Lloyd George, he was shouted down by "Newport Englishmen" in the meeting, although the <i><a href="/wiki/South_Wales_Argus" title="South Wales Argus">South Wales Argus</a></i> suggested the poor crowd behaviour came from Lloyd George's supporters.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Following difficulty in uniting the Liberal federations along with <span title="Welsh-language text"><span lang="cy" style="font-style: normal;">Cymru Fydd</span></span> in the South East and thus, difficulty in gaining support for Home Rule for Wales, Lloyd George shifted his focus to improving the socio-economic environment of Wales as part of the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">United Kingdom</a> and the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>. Although Lloyd George considered himself a "Welshman first", he saw the opportunities for Wales within the UK.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_24-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Uniting_Welsh_Liberals">Uniting Welsh Liberals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Uniting Welsh Liberals">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1898, Lloyd George created the Welsh National Liberal Council, a loose umbrella organisation covering the two federations, but with very little power. In time, it became known as the Liberal Party of Wales.<sup id="cite_ref-alderton_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-alderton-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Support_of_Welsh_institutions">Support of Welsh institutions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Support of Welsh institutions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Lloyd George had a connection to or promoted the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/National_Library_of_Wales" title="National Library of Wales">National Library of Wales</a>, the <a href="/wiki/National_Museum_of_Wales" class="mw-redirect" title="National Museum of Wales">National Museum of Wales</a> and the Welsh Department of the <a href="/wiki/Board_of_education" title="Board of education">Board of Education</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> He also showed considerable support for the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Wales" title="University of Wales">University of Wales</a>, that its establishment raised the status of Welsh people and that the university deserved greater funding by the UK government.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_to_the_Boer_War">Opposition to the Boer War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Opposition to the Boer War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Lloyd George had been impressed by his journey to Canada in 1899. Although sometimes wrongly supposed—both at the time and subsequently—to be a <a href="/wiki/Little_Englander" title="Little Englander">Little Englander</a>, he was not an opponent of the British Empire <i>per se</i>, but in a speech at Birkenhead (21 November 1901) he stressed that it needed to be based on freedom, including for India, not "racial arrogance".<sup id="cite_ref-GriggCrisisAtSea_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GriggCrisisAtSea-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 61">&#58;&#8202;61&#8202;</span></sup> Consequently, he gained national fame by displaying vehement opposition to the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Second Boer War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHattersley2010ch._&quot;Go_for_Joe&quot;,_pp._&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsisbn_9781408700976page119_119&#93;–144_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHattersley2010ch._&quot;Go_for_Joe&quot;,_pp._[httpsarchiveorgdetailsisbn_9781408700976page119_119]–144-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following Rosebery's lead, he based his attack firstly on what were supposed to be Britain's war aims—remedying the grievances of the <span title="Afrikaans-language text"><i lang="af">italicno</i></span> and in particular the claim that they were wrongly denied the right to vote, saying "I do not believe the war has any connection with the franchise. It is a question of 45% dividends" and that England (which did not then have universal male suffrage) was more in need of franchise reform than the Boer republics. A second attack came on the cost of the war, which, he argued, prevented overdue social reform in England, such as old-age pensions and workmen's cottages. As the fighting continued his attacks moved to its conduct by the generals, who, he said (basing his words on reports by <a href="/wiki/William_Burdett-Coutts" title="William Burdett-Coutts">William Burdett-Coutts</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Times" title="The Times">The Times</a></i>), were not providing for the sick or wounded soldiers and were starving Boer women and children in concentration camps. But his major thrusts were reserved for the Chamberlains, accusing them of <a href="/wiki/War_profiteering" title="War profiteering">war profiteering</a> through the family company <a href="/wiki/Kynoch" title="Kynoch">Kynoch</a> Ltd, of which Chamberlain's brother was chairman. The firm had won tenders to the <a href="/wiki/War_Office" title="War Office">War Office</a>, though its prices were higher than some of its competitors. After speaking at a meeting in Birmingham Lloyd George had to be smuggled out disguised as a policeman, as his life was in danger from the mob. At this time the Liberal Party was badly split as <a href="/wiki/H._H._Asquith" title="H. H. Asquith">H. H. Asquith</a>, <a href="/wiki/Richard_Haldane,_1st_Viscount_Haldane" title="Richard Haldane, 1st Viscount Haldane">R. B. Haldane</a> and others were supporters of the war and formed the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Imperialists" title="Liberal Imperialists">Liberal Imperial League</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGilbert1987ch._The_Free_Lancer:_The_South_African_War,_1895–1902,_pp._178–214_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilbert1987ch._The_Free_Lancer:_The_South_African_War,_1895–1902,_pp._178–214-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_to_the_Education_Act_1902">Opposition to the Education Act 1902</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Opposition to the Education Act 1902">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Lloyd_George_1902.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/David_Lloyd_George_1902.jpg/170px-David_Lloyd_George_1902.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="237" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/David_Lloyd_George_1902.jpg/255px-David_Lloyd_George_1902.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/David_Lloyd_George_1902.jpg/340px-David_Lloyd_George_1902.jpg 2x" data-file-width="575" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption>Lloyd George in 1902</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 24 March <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Balfour" title="Arthur Balfour">Arthur Balfour</a>, just about to take office as Prime Minister, introduced a bill which was to become the <a href="/wiki/Education_Act_1902" title="Education Act 1902">Education Act 1902</a>. Lloyd George supported the bill's proposals to bring voluntary schools (i.e. religious schools—mainly Church of England, and some Roman Catholic schools in certain inner city areas) in England and Wales under the control of local school boards, who would conduct inspections and appoint two out of each school's six managers. However, other measures were more contentious: the majority-religious school managers would retain the power to employ or sack teachers on religious grounds and would receive money from the <a href="/wiki/Rates_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Rates in the United Kingdom">rates</a> (local property taxes). This offended nonconformist opinion, then in a period of revival, as it seemed like a return to the hated <a href="/wiki/Church_rates" class="mw-redirect" title="Church rates">church rates</a> (which had been compulsory until 1868), and inspired a large grassroots campaign against the bill.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyWithRadical_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyWithRadical-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 52–7">&#58;&#8202;52–7&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Within days of the bill's unveiling (27 March), Lloyd George denounced "priestcraft" in a speech to his constituents, and he began an active campaign of speaking against the bill, both in public in Wales (with a few speeches in England) and in the House of Commons. On 12 November, Balfour accepted an amendment (willingly, but a rare case of him doing so), ostensibly from <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Thomas,_1st_Baron_Pontypridd" title="Alfred Thomas, 1st Baron Pontypridd">Alfred Thomas</a>, chairman of the Welsh Parliamentary Liberal Party, but in reality instigated by Lloyd George, transferring control of Welsh schools from appointed boards to the elected county councils. The Education Act became law on 20 December 1902.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyWithRadical_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyWithRadical-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 52–7">&#58;&#8202;52–7&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Lloyd George now announced the real purpose of the amendment, described as a "booby trap" by his biographer John Grigg. The Welsh National Liberal Council soon adopted his proposal that county councils should refuse funding unless repairs were carried out to schools (many were in a poor state), and should also demand control of school governing bodies and a ban on religious tests for teachers; "no control, no cash" was Lloyd George's slogan. Lloyd George negotiated with <a href="/wiki/A._G._Edwards_(bishop)" title="A. G. Edwards (bishop)">A. G. Edwards</a>, Anglican <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_St_Asaph" title="Bishop of St Asaph">Bishop of St Asaph</a>, and was prepared to settle on an "agreed religious syllabus" or even to allow Anglican teaching in schools, provided the county councils retained control of teacher appointments, but this compromise failed after opposition from other Anglican Welsh bishops. A well-attended meeting at Park Hall Cardiff (3 June 1903) passed a number of resolutions by acclamation: county council control of schools, withholding money from schools or even withholding rates from unsupportive county councils. The Liberals soon gained control of all thirteen Welsh County Councils. Lloyd George continued to speak in England against the bill, but the campaign there was less aggressively led, taking the form of passive resistance to rate paying.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyWithRadical_42-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyWithRadical-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 52–57">&#58;&#8202;52–57&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>In August 1904 the government brought in the Education (Local Authority Default) Act giving the Board of Education power to take charge of schools, which Lloyd George immediately nicknamed the "Coercion of Wales Act". He addressed another convention in Cardiff on 6 October 1904, during which he proclaimed that the Welsh flag was "a dragon rampant,<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> not a sheep recumbent". Under his leadership, the convention pledged not to maintain elementary schools, or to withdraw children from elementary schools altogether so that they could be taught privately by the nonconformist churches. In Travis Crosbie's words, public resistance to the Education Act had caused a "perfect impasse".<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyWithRadical_42-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyWithRadical-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 52–57">&#58;&#8202;52–57&#8202;</span></sup> There was no progress between Welsh counties and Westminster until 1905.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Having already gained national recognition for his anti-Boer War campaigns, Lloyd George's leadership of the attacks on the Education Act gave him a strong parliamentary reputation and marked him as a likely future cabinet member.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHattersley2010ch._&quot;Noncomformity&#39;s_Champion&quot;,_pp._&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsisbn_9781408700976page145_145&#93;–165_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHattersley2010ch._&quot;Noncomformity&#39;s_Champion&quot;,_pp._[httpsarchiveorgdetailsisbn_9781408700976page145_145]–165-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The Act served to reunify the Liberals after their divisions over the Boer War and to increase Nonconformist influence in the party, which then included educational reform as policy in the <a href="/wiki/1906_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1906 United Kingdom general election">1906 election</a>, which resulted in a Liberal landslide.<sup id="cite_ref-Daglish1994_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Daglish1994-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> All 34 Welsh seats returned a Liberal, except for one Labour seat in Merthyr Tydfil.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_stances">Other stances</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Other stances">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Lloyd George also supported the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Romantic_Nationalist&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Romantic Nationalist (page does not exist)">Romantic Nationalist</a> idea of <a href="/wiki/Pan-Celticism" title="Pan-Celticism">Pan-Celtic unity</a> and gave a speech at the 1904 <a href="/wiki/Pan-Celtic_Congress" class="mw-redirect" title="Pan-Celtic Congress">Pan-Celtic Congress</a> in <a href="/wiki/Caernarfon" title="Caernarfon">Caernarfon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his second-ever speech in the House of Commons, Lloyd George criticised the grandeur of the monarchy.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lloyd George wrote extensively for Liberal-supporting papers such as the <i><a href="/wiki/Manchester_Guardian" class="mw-redirect" title="Manchester Guardian">Manchester Guardian</a></i> and spoke on Liberal issues (particularly temperance—the "<a href="/wiki/Local_option" title="Local option">local option</a>"—and national as opposed to denominational education) throughout England and Wales.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_24-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He served as the legal adviser of <a href="/wiki/Theodor_Herzl" title="Theodor Herzl">Theodor Herzl</a> in his negotiations with the British government regarding the <a href="/wiki/Uganda_Scheme" title="Uganda Scheme">Uganda Scheme</a>, proposed as an alternative homeland for the Jews due to Turkish refusal to grant a charter for Jewish settlement in Palestine.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="President_of_the_Board_of_Trade_.281905.E2.80.931908.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="President_of_the_Board_of_Trade_(1905–1908)">President of the Board of Trade (1905–1908)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: President of the Board of Trade (1905–1908)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Education_Act_1902#The_failed_Education_Bill_of_1906" title="Education Act 1902">Education Act 1902 §&#160;The failed Education Bill of 1906</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ChurchillGeorge0001.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/ChurchillGeorge0001.jpg/170px-ChurchillGeorge0001.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="215" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/ChurchillGeorge0001.jpg/255px-ChurchillGeorge0001.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/ChurchillGeorge0001.jpg/340px-ChurchillGeorge0001.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1808" data-file-height="2284" /></a><figcaption>Lloyd George and <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> in 1907</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1905, Lloyd George entered the new Liberal Cabinet of <a href="/wiki/Sir_Henry_Campbell-Bannerman" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman">Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman</a> as <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Board_of_Trade" title="President of the Board of Trade">President of the Board of Trade</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyWithRadical_42-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyWithRadical-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 63">&#58;&#8202;63&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>The first priority on taking office was the repeal of the 1902 Education Act. Lloyd George took the lead along with <a href="/wiki/Augustine_Birrell" title="Augustine Birrell">Augustine Birrell</a>, President of the Board of Education. Lloyd George appears to have been the dominant figure on the committee drawing up the bill in its later stages and insisted that the bill create a separate education committee for Wales. Birrell complained privately that the bill, introduced in the Commons on 9 April 1906, owed more to Lloyd George and that he himself had had little say in its contents.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyIntheCabinet_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyIntheCabinet-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 74–77">&#58;&#8202;74–77&#8202;</span></sup> The bill passed the House of Commons greatly amended but was completely mangled by the House of Lords.<sup id="cite_ref-Daglish1994_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Daglish1994-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> For the rest of the year Lloyd George made numerous public speeches attacking the House of Lords for mutilating the bill with wrecking amendments, in defiance of the Liberals' electoral mandate to reform the 1902 Act. Lloyd George was rebuked by King <a href="/wiki/Edward_VII" title="Edward VII">Edward VII</a> for these speeches: the Prime Minister defended him to the King's secretary <a href="/wiki/Francis_Knollys,_1st_Viscount_Knollys" title="Francis Knollys, 1st Viscount Knollys">Francis Knollys</a>, stating that his behaviour in Parliament was more constructive but that in speeches to the public "the combative spirit seems to get the better of him".<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyIntheCabinet_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyIntheCabinet-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 74–77">&#58;&#8202;74–77&#8202;</span></sup> No compromise was possible and the bill was abandoned, allowing the 1902 Act to continue in effect.<sup id="cite_ref-Daglish1994_46-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Daglish1994-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> As a result of Lloyd George's lobbying, a separate department for Wales<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> was created within the Board of Education.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyIntheCabinet_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyIntheCabinet-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 74–77">&#58;&#8202;74–77&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Nonconformists were bitterly upset by the failure of the Liberal Party to reform the 1902 Education Act, its most important promise to them, and over time their support for the Liberal Party slowly fell away.<sup id="cite_ref-Richards1972_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Richards1972-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the Board of Trade Lloyd George introduced legislation on many topics, from <a href="/wiki/Merchant_Shipping_Act_1906" title="Merchant Shipping Act 1906">merchant shipping</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Port_of_London_Act_1908" title="Port of London Act 1908">Port of London</a> to <a href="/wiki/Companies_Act_1907" title="Companies Act 1907">companies</a> and railway regulation. His main achievement was in stopping a proposed national strike of the railway unions by brokering an agreement between the unions and the railway companies. While almost all the companies refused to recognise the unions, Lloyd George persuaded the companies to recognise elected representatives of the workers who sat with the company representatives on conciliation boards—one for each company. If those boards failed to agree then an arbitrator would be called upon.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyIntheCabinet_50-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyIntheCabinet-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 69–73">&#58;&#8202;69–73&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer_.281908.E2.80.931915.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer_(1908–1915)">Chancellor of the Exchequer (1908–1915)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Chancellor of the Exchequer (1908–1915)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Women%27s_suffrage_in_Wales#David_Lloyd_George_and_the_suffrage_movement,_1907–1912" title="Women&#39;s suffrage in Wales">David Lloyd George and the suffrage movement, 1907–1912</a></div>
<p>On Campbell-Bannerman's death, he succeeded Asquith, who had become prime minister, as <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">Chancellor of the Exchequer</a> from 1908 to 1915.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyIntheCabinet_50-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyIntheCabinet-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 81">&#58;&#8202;81&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyRighteousness_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyRighteousness-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 189–190">&#58;&#8202;189–190&#8202;</span></sup> While he continued some work from the Board of Trade—for example, legislation to establish the <a href="/wiki/Port_of_London_Authority" title="Port of London Authority">Port of London Authority</a> and to pursue traditional Liberal programmes such as licensing law reforms—his first major trial in this role was over the 1909–1910 Naval Estimates. The Liberal manifesto at the <a href="/wiki/1906_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1906 United Kingdom general election">1906 general election</a> included a commitment to reduce military expenditure. Lloyd George strongly supported this, writing to <a href="/wiki/Reginald_McKenna" title="Reginald McKenna">Reginald McKenna</a>, First Lord of the Admiralty, of "the emphatic pledges given by all of us at the last general election to reduce the gigantic expenditure on armaments built up by the recklessness of our predecessors." He then proposed the programme be reduced from six to four <a href="/wiki/Dreadnoughts" class="mw-redirect" title="Dreadnoughts">dreadnoughts</a>. This was adopted by the government, but there was a public storm when the Conservatives, with covert support from the First Sea Lord, Admiral <a href="/wiki/John_Fisher,_1st_Baron_Fisher" title="John Fisher, 1st Baron Fisher">Jackie Fisher</a>, campaigned for more with the slogan "We want eight and we won't wait". This resulted in Lloyd George's defeat in Cabinet and the adoption of estimates including provision for eight dreadnoughts.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> During this period he was also a target of protest by the women's suffrage movement, for he professed personal support for extension of the suffrage but did not move for changes within the Parliament process.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Lloyd_George_1911.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/David_Lloyd_George_1911.jpg/170px-David_Lloyd_George_1911.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="235" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/David_Lloyd_George_1911.jpg/255px-David_Lloyd_George_1911.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/David_Lloyd_George_1911.jpg/340px-David_Lloyd_George_1911.jpg 2x" data-file-width="543" data-file-height="750" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Chancellor Lloyd George by <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Williams_(Welsh_artist)" title="Christopher Williams (Welsh artist)">Christopher Williams</a> (1911)</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span id="People.27s_Budget.2C_1909"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="People's_Budget,_1909">People's Budget, 1909</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: People&#039;s Budget, 1909">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Budget" title="People&#39;s Budget">People's Budget</a></div>
<p>In 1909, Lloyd George introduced his <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Budget" title="People&#39;s Budget">People's Budget</a>, imposing a 20% <a href="/wiki/Land_value_tax" title="Land value tax">tax on the unearned increase in the value of land</a>, payable at the death of the owner or sale of the land, and <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1154941027">.mw-parser-output .frac{white-space:nowrap}.mw-parser-output .frac .num,.mw-parser-output .frac .den{font-size:80%;line-height:0;vertical-align:super}.mw-parser-output .frac .den{vertical-align:sub}.mw-parser-output .sr-only{border:0;clip:rect(0,0,0,0);clip-path:polygon(0px 0px,0px 0px,0px 0px);height:1px;margin:-1px;overflow:hidden;padding:0;position:absolute;width:1px}</style><span class="frac" role="math"><span class="num">1</span>&#8260;<span class="den">2</span></span>&#160;d. on undeveloped land and minerals, increased death duties, a rise in income tax, and the introduction of <a href="/wiki/Supertax" class="mw-redirect" title="Supertax">Supertax</a> on income over £3,000.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcKinstry2005504–505_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcKinstry2005504–505-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> There were taxes also on luxuries, <a href="/wiki/Alcohol_(drug)" title="Alcohol (drug)">alcohol</a> and tobacco, so that money could be made available for the new welfare programmes as well as new battleships. The nation's landowners (well represented in the House of Lords) were intensely angry at the new taxes, mostly at the proposed very high tax on land values, but also because the instrumental redistribution of wealth could be used to detract from an argument for protective tariffs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamsden1998&#91;&#91;Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_May_2018&#93;&#93;&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;&#91;&#91;Wikipedia:Citing_sources&#124;&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(May_2018)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;&#93;&#93;&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamsden1998[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_May_2018]]&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(May_2018)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;]]&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The immediate consequences included the end of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_League_(United_Kingdom)" title="Liberal League (United Kingdom)">Liberal League</a>, and Rosebery breaking friendship with the Liberal Party, which in itself was for Lloyd George a triumph. He had won the case of social reform without losing the debate on Free Trade.<sup id="cite_ref-JenkinsDLG_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JenkinsDLG-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 166">&#58;&#8202;166&#8202;</span></sup> <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Balfour" title="Arthur Balfour">Arthur Balfour</a> denounced the budget as "vindictive, inequitable, based on no principles, and injurious to the productive capacity of the country."<sup id="cite_ref-JenkinsDLG_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JenkinsDLG-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 167">&#58;&#8202;167&#8202;</span></sup> <a href="/wiki/Roy_Jenkins" title="Roy Jenkins">Roy Jenkins</a> described it as the most reverberating since Gladstone's in 1860.<sup id="cite_ref-JenkinsDLG_58-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JenkinsDLG-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 172">&#58;&#8202;172&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>In the House of Commons, Lloyd George gave a brilliant account of the budget, which was attacked by the Conservatives. On the stump, notably at his Limehouse speech in 1909, he denounced the Conservatives and the wealthy classes with all his very considerable oratorical power. In a break with <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_convention_(political_custom)" title="Constitutional convention (political custom)">convention</a>, the budget was defeated by the Conservative majority in the House of Lords. The elections of 1910 narrowly upheld the Liberal government. The 1909 budget was passed on 28 April 1910 by the Lords and received the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Assent" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Assent">Royal Assent</a> on the 29th.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHattersley2010ch._&quot;A_Reasonable_Way_Out&quot;,_pp._&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsisbn_9781408700976page278_278&#93;–279_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHattersley2010ch._&quot;A_Reasonable_Way_Out&quot;,_pp._[httpsarchiveorgdetailsisbn_9781408700976page278_278]–279-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Subsequently, the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Act_1911" title="Parliament Act 1911">Parliament Act 1911</a> removed the House of Lords' power to block money bills, and with a few exceptions replaced their veto power over most bills with a power to delay them for up to two years.
</p><p>Although old-age pensions had already been introduced by Asquith as Chancellor, Lloyd George was largely responsible for the introduction of state financial support for the sick and infirm (known colloquially as "going on the Lloyd George" for decades afterwards)—legislation referred to as the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Reforms" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Reforms">Liberal Reforms</a>. Lloyd George also succeeded in putting through Parliament his <a href="/wiki/National_Insurance_Act_1911" title="National Insurance Act 1911">National Insurance Act 1911</a>, making provision for sickness and invalidism, and a system of unemployment insurance. He was helped in his endeavours by forty or so backbenchers who regularly pushed for new social measures, often voted with Labour MPs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatts2002&#91;&#91;Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_May_2018&#93;&#93;&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;&#91;&#91;Wikipedia:Citing_sources&#124;&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(May_2018)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;&#93;&#93;&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatts2002[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_May_2018]]&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(May_2018)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;]]&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> These social reforms in Britain were the beginnings of a <a href="/wiki/Welfare_state" title="Welfare state">welfare state</a> and fulfilled the aim of dampening down the demands of the growing working class for rather more radical solutions to their impoverishment.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Under his leadership, after 1909 the Liberals extended minimum wages to farmworkers.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Lloyd_George_c1911.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/David_Lloyd_George_c1911.jpg/220px-David_Lloyd_George_c1911.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="298" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/David_Lloyd_George_c1911.jpg/330px-David_Lloyd_George_c1911.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/David_Lloyd_George_c1911.jpg/440px-David_Lloyd_George_c1911.jpg 2x" data-file-width="460" data-file-height="624" /></a><figcaption>David Lloyd George circa 1911</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span id="Mansion_House_Speech.2C_1911"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Mansion_House_Speech,_1911">Mansion House Speech, 1911</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Mansion House Speech, 1911">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3><p>
Lloyd George was an opponent of warfare but he paid little attention to foreign affairs until the <a href="/wiki/Second_Moroccan_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Moroccan Crisis">Agadir Crisis</a> of 1911. After consulting Edward Grey (the foreign minister) and H.<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>H. Asquith (the prime minister) he gave a stirring and patriotic speech at <a href="/wiki/Mansion_House,_London" title="Mansion House, London">Mansion House</a> on 21 July 1911.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> He stated:</p><blockquote><p> But if a situation were to be forced upon us in which peace could only be preserved by the surrender of the great and beneficent position Britain has won by centuries of heroism and achievement, by allowing Britain to be treated where her interests were vitally affected as if she were of no account in the Cabinet of nations, then I say emphatically that peace at that price would be a humiliation intolerable for a great country like ours to endure. National honour is no party question. The security of our great international trade is no party question.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> </p></blockquote>
<p>He was warning both France and Germany, but the public response cheered solidarity with France and hostility toward Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Berlin was outraged, blaming Lloyd George for doing "untold harm both with regard to German public opinion and the negotiations." <a href="/wiki/Paul_Wolff_Metternich" title="Paul Wolff Metternich">Count Metternich</a>, Germany's ambassador in London, said, "Mr Lloyd George's speech came upon us like a thunderbolt".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrey1925i,_pp._236–237_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrey1925i,_pp._236–237-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Marconi_scandal_1913">Marconi scandal 1913</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Marconi scandal 1913">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1913, Lloyd George, along with <a href="/wiki/Rufus_Isaacs,_1st_Marquess_of_Reading" title="Rufus Isaacs, 1st Marquess of Reading">Rufus Isaacs</a>, the Attorney General, was involved in the <a href="/wiki/Marconi_scandal" title="Marconi scandal">Marconi scandal</a>. Accused of speculating in Marconi shares on the inside information that they were about to be awarded a key government contract (which would have caused them to increase in value), he told the House of Commons that he had not speculated in the shares of "that company". He had in fact bought shares in the American Marconi Company.<sup id="cite_ref-Pugh1988p59-61_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pugh1988p59-61-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Welsh_Disestablishment">Welsh Disestablishment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Welsh Disestablishment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Lloyd George was instrumental in fulfilling a long-standing aspiration to disestablish the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican Church">Anglican Church</a> of Wales. As with <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Ireland_Act_1914" title="Government of Ireland Act 1914">Irish Home Rule</a>, previous attempts to enact this had failed in the 1892–1895 Governments, and were now made possible by the removal of the Lords' veto in 1911, and as with Home Rule the initial bill (1912) was delayed for two years by the Lords, becoming <a href="/wiki/Welsh_Church_Act_1914" title="Welsh Church Act 1914">law in 1914</a>, only to be <a href="/wiki/Suspensory_Act_1914" title="Suspensory Act 1914">suspended for the duration of the war</a>. After the <a href="/wiki/Welsh_Church_(Temporalities)_Act_1919" title="Welsh Church (Temporalities) Act 1919">Welsh Church (Temporalities) Act 1919</a> was passed, Welsh Disestablishment finally <a href="/wiki/Came_into_force" class="mw-redirect" title="Came into force">came into force</a> in 1920. This Act also removed the right of the six Welsh Bishops in the new <a href="/wiki/Church_in_Wales" title="Church in Wales">Church in Wales</a> to sit in the House of Lords and removed (disendowed) certain pre-1662 property rights.<sup id="cite_ref-TomJonesMember_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TomJonesMember-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 18–20">&#58;&#8202;18–20&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-TomJonesCabinet_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TomJonesCabinet-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 42">&#58;&#8202;42&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="First_World_War">First World War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: First World War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
