<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1918_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1918 United Kingdom general election">1918 United Kingdom general election</a></div>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/1918_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1918 United Kingdom general election">"Coupon election" of December 1918</a> he led a coalition of Conservatives and his own faction of Liberals to a landslide victory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETurner1992317–333_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETurner1992317–333-132">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Coalition candidates received a "<a href="/wiki/Coalition_coupon" class="mw-redirect" title="Coalition coupon">coalition coupon</a>" (an endorsement letter signed by Lloyd George and Law). He did not say "We shall squeeze the German lemon until the pips squeak" (that was <a href="/wiki/Sir_Eric_Geddes" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Eric Geddes">Sir Eric Geddes</a>), but he did express that sentiment about reparations from Germany to pay the entire cost of the war, including pensions. He said that German industrial capacity "will go a pretty long way". We must have "the uttermost farthing", and "shall search their pockets for it".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERose199914–15_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERose199914–15-133">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> As the campaign closed, he summarised his programme:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHavighurst1985149_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHavighurst1985149-134">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ol><li>Trial of the <a href="/wiki/William_II,_German_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="William II, German Emperor">exiled Kaiser Wilhelm II</a>;</li>
<li>Punishment of those guilty of atrocities;</li>
<li>Fullest indemnity from Germany;</li>
<li>Britain for the British, socially and industrially;</li>
<li>Rehabilitation of those broken in the war; and</li>
<li>A happier country for all.</li></ol>
<p>The election was fought not so much on the peace issue and what to do with Germany, although those themes played a role. More important was the voters' evaluation of Lloyd George in terms of what he had accomplished so far and what he promised for the future. His supporters emphasised that he had won the Great War. Against his strong record in social legislation, he himself called for making "a country fit for heroes to live in".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor1976127–128_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor1976127–128-135">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Coalition gained an overwhelming victory, winning 525 of the 707 seats contested; however, the Conservatives had more than two-thirds of the Coalition's seats. Asquith's independent Liberals were crushed, although they were still the official opposition as the two Liberal factions combined had more seats than Labour.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHavighurst1966151_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHavighurst1966151-136">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Accounts vary about the factional allegiance of some MPs: by some accounts as few as 29 uncouponed Liberals had been elected, only 3 with any junior ministerial experience, and only 23 of them were actually opponents of the coalition. Until April 1919 the government whip was extended to <i>all</i> Liberal MPs and Lloyd George might easily have been elected chairman of the Liberal MPs (Asquith was still party leader but had lost his seat) had he been willing to antagonise his Conservative coalition partners by doing so.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKoss1985241–2_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoss1985241–2-137">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Paris_1919">Paris 1919</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=39" title="Edit section: Paris 1919">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ClemenceauLloydGeorgeYOrlando.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/ClemenceauLloydGeorgeYOrlando.jpg/220px-ClemenceauLloydGeorgeYOrlando.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="209" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/ClemenceauLloydGeorgeYOrlando.jpg/330px-ClemenceauLloydGeorgeYOrlando.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/ClemenceauLloydGeorgeYOrlando.jpg/440px-ClemenceauLloydGeorgeYOrlando.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2852" data-file-height="2708" /></a><figcaption>Georges Clemenceau, David Lloyd George and Vittorio Orlando at Paris</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Big_four.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Big_four.jpg/220px-Big_four.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="169" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Big_four.jpg/330px-Big_four.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Big_four.jpg/440px-Big_four.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2616" data-file-height="2008" /></a><figcaption>"The Big Four" made all the major decisions at the Paris Peace Conference (from left to right, Lloyd George, <a href="/wiki/Vittorio_Emanuele_Orlando" title="Vittorio Emanuele Orlando">Vittorio Emanuele Orlando</a> of Italy, <a href="/wiki/Georges_Clemenceau" title="Georges Clemenceau">Georges Clemenceau</a> of France, <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson" title="Woodrow Wilson">Woodrow Wilson</a> of the US)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Lloyd George represented Britain at the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference,_1919" class="mw-redirect" title="Paris Peace Conference, 1919">Paris Peace Conference</a>, clashing with French Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Georges_Clemenceau" title="Georges Clemenceau">Georges Clemenceau</a>, US President <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson" title="Woodrow Wilson">Woodrow Wilson</a>, and Italian Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Vittorio_Orlando" class="mw-redirect" title="Vittorio Orlando">Vittorio Orlando</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacMillan2001&#91;&#91;Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_May_2018&#93;&#93;&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;&#91;&#91;Wikipedia:Citing_sources&#124;&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(May_2018)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;&#93;&#93;&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacMillan2001[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_May_2018]]&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(May_2018)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;]]&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;-138">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Unlike Clemenceau and Orlando, Lloyd George on the whole stood on the side of generosity and moderation. He did not want to utterly destroy the German economy and political system—as Clemenceau demanded—with massive reparations. The economist <a href="/wiki/John_Maynard_Keynes" title="John Maynard Keynes">John Maynard Keynes</a> looked askance at Lloyd George's economic credentials in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Economic_Consequences_of_the_Peace" title="The Economic Consequences of the Peace">The Economic Consequences of the Peace</a></i>,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and in <i>Essays in Biography</i> called the Prime Minister a "goat-footed bard, half-human visitor to our age from the hag-ridden magic and enchanted woods of <a href="/wiki/Celts" title="Celts">Celtic</a> antiquity".<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lloyd George was also responsible for the pro-German shift in the peace conditions regarding borders of Poland. Instead of handing over <a href="/wiki/Upper_Silesia_plebiscite" class="mw-redirect" title="Upper Silesia plebiscite">Upper Silesia</a> (2,073,000 people), and the <a href="/wiki/East_Prussian_plebiscite" class="mw-redirect" title="East Prussian plebiscite">southern part of East Prussia</a> (720,000 people) to Poland as was planned before, the plebiscite was organised. <a href="/wiki/Danzig" class="mw-redirect" title="Danzig">Danzig</a> (366,000 people) was organised as the <a href="/wiki/Free_City_of_Danzig" title="Free City of Danzig">Free City of Danzig</a>. The Poles were grateful that he had saved that country from the Bolsheviks but were annoyed by his comment that they were "children who gave trouble".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavies1971132–154_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavies1971132–154-140">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Distrusting Foreign Office professionals, Lloyd George and his team at Paris instead relied on non-professional experts through informal networks below them. They consulted with <a href="/wiki/James_Headlam-Morley" title="James Headlam-Morley">James Headlam-Morley</a> about Danzig. Several academic historians also were consulted. Their experiences were the basis for building up diplomatic history as a field of academic research and the emergence of the new academic discipline of international relations.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Asked how he had done at the peace conference, Lloyd George retorted: "I think I did as well as might be expected, seated as I was between Jesus Christ [Wilson] and Napoleon Bonaparte [Clemenceau]."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECashman1988526_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECashman1988526-142">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Historian Antony Lentin evaluated his role in Paris as a major success, saying:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>He was an unrivalled negotiator: on top of his brief, full of bounce, sure of himself, forceful, engaging, compelling.&#160;... Acutely sensitive to what he divined as the motive force in his listeners, he was adept at finding the right tone and turn of phrase to divert that force in the desired direction.&#160;... [he had] powerful combative instincts, executive drive and an indomitable determination to succeed.&#160;... [He secured] as visible and immediate trophies&#160;... the spoils of empire: the coveted Middle Eastern mandates, protecting the route to India and rich in oil. There were the confiscated German colonies in Africa and the South Pacific, making a reality of British rule from Cairo to the Cape and setting the far-flung bounds of Empire at their widest.&#160;... [while being] wholly in accord with British interest in a continental balance of power.<sup id="cite_ref-LentinMarch1995_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LentinMarch1995-143">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Postwar_social_reforms">Postwar social reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=40" title="Edit section: Postwar social reforms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>A major programme of social reform was introduced under Lloyd George in the last months of the war, and in the post-war years. The Workmen's Compensation (Silicosis) Act 1918 (which was introduced a year later) allowed for compensation to be paid to men "who could prove they had worked in rock which contained no less than 80% silica."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcIvorJohnston200774_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcIvorJohnston200774-144">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Education_Act_1918" title="Education Act 1918">Education Act 1918</a> raised the school leaving age to 14, increased the powers and duties of the Board of Education (together with the money it could provide to Local Education Authorities), and introduced a system of compulsory part-time continuation schools for children between the ages of 14 and 16.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe201451_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe201451-145">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Blind_Persons_Act_1920" title="Blind Persons Act 1920">Blind Persons Act 1920</a> provided assistance for unemployed blind people and blind persons who were in low paid employment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomasSmith200813–14_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomasSmith200813–14-146">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Housing_and_Town_Planning_Act_1919" class="mw-redirect" title="Housing and Town Planning Act 1919">Housing and Town Planning Act 1919</a> provided subsidies for house building by local authorities, and 170,000 dwellings were built under it by the end of 1922.<sup id="cite_ref-Thane1996p136_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thane1996p136-147">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> which established, according to <a href="/wiki/A._J._P._Taylor" title="A. J. P. Taylor">A. J. P. Taylor</a>, "the principle that housing was a social service".<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> A further 30,000 houses were constructed by private enterprise with government subsidy under a second act.<sup id="cite_ref-Thane1996p136_147-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thane1996p136-147">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
The <a href="/wiki/Land_Settlement_(Facilities)_Act_1919" title="Land Settlement (Facilities) Act 1919">Land Settlement (Facilities) Act 1919</a> and Land Settlement (Scotland) Acts of 1919 encouraged local authorities to provide land for people to take up farming "and also to provide allotments in urban areas."
</p><p>The Rent Act 1920 was intended to safeguard working-class tenants against exorbitant rent increases, but it failed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELowe1984&#91;&#91;Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_October_2018&#93;&#93;&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;&#91;&#91;Wikipedia:Citing_sources&#124;&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(October_2018)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;&#93;&#93;&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELowe1984[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_October_2018]]&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(October_2018)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;]]&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;-149">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> Rent controls were continued after the war, and an "out-of-work donation" was introduced for ex-servicemen and civilians.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Electoral_changes:_suffragism">Electoral changes: suffragism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=41" title="Edit section: Electoral changes: suffragism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Women%27s_suffrage_in_Wales#David_Lloyd_George_and_the_suffrage_movement,_1907–1912" title="Women&#39;s suffrage in Wales">Women's suffrage in Wales §&#160;David Lloyd George and the suffrage movement, 1907–1912</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Representation_of_the_People_Act_1918" title="Representation of the People Act 1918">Representation of the People Act 1918</a> greatly extended the franchise for men (by abolishing most property qualifications) and gave the vote to many women over 30, and the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_(Qualification_of_Women)_Act_1918" title="Parliament (Qualification of Women) Act 1918">Parliament (Qualification of Women) Act 1918</a> enabled women to sit in the House of Commons. The <a href="/wiki/Sex_Disqualification_(Removal)_Act_1919" title="Sex Disqualification (Removal) Act 1919">Sex Disqualification (Removal) Act 1919</a> provided that "A person shall not be disqualified by sex or marriage from the exercise of any public function, or from being appointed to or holding any civil or judicial office or post, or from entering or assuming or carrying on any civil profession or vocation, or for admission to any incorporated society".
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Wages_for_workers">Wages for workers</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=42" title="Edit section: Wages for workers">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Unemployment_Insurance_Act_1920" title="Unemployment Insurance Act 1920">Unemployment Insurance Act 1920</a> extended <a href="/wiki/National_insurance" class="mw-redirect" title="National insurance">national insurance</a> to 11 million additional workers. This was considered to be a revolutionary measure, in that it extended unemployment insurance to almost the entire labour force, whereas only certain categories of workers had been covered before.<sup id="cite_ref-Davies1994p510_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davies1994p510-151">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> As a result of this legislation, roughly three-quarters of the British workforce were now covered by unemployment insurance.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Crown_Prince_Hirohito_and_Lloyd_George_1921.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Crown_Prince_Hirohito_and_Lloyd_George_1921.jpg/240px-Crown_Prince_Hirohito_and_Lloyd_George_1921.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="183" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Crown_Prince_Hirohito_and_Lloyd_George_1921.jpg/360px-Crown_Prince_Hirohito_and_Lloyd_George_1921.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Crown_Prince_Hirohito_and_Lloyd_George_1921.jpg/480px-Crown_Prince_Hirohito_and_Lloyd_George_1921.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1063" data-file-height="809" /></a><figcaption>Lloyd George with Japanese Prince <a href="/wiki/Hirohito" title="Hirohito">Hirohito</a>, 1921</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Agriculture_Act_1920" title="Agriculture Act 1920">Agriculture Act 1920</a> provided for farm labourers to receive a minimum wage while the state continued to guarantee the prices of farm produce until 1921. It also provided tenant farmers with greater protection by granting them better security of tenure.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-153">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2018)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In education, teachers' salaries were standardised, and more than doubled from pre-War levels, in 1921 by the <a href="/wiki/Burnham_Committee" class="mw-redirect" title="Burnham Committee">Burnham Committee</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Pugh1988p139_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pugh1988p139-154">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/w/index.php?title=Mining_Industry_Act_1920&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Mining Industry Act 1920 (page does not exist)">Mining Industry Act 1920</a> placed a mandatory requirement to provide social welfare opportunities to mining communities,<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> while the Public Health (Tuberculosis) Act 1921 increased the obligation of local authorities to treat and prevent TB.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorpe201454_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorpe201454-156">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Health_reforms">Health reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=43" title="Edit section: Health reforms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1919, the government set up the <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Health_(United_Kingdom)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ministry of Health (United Kingdom)">Ministry of Health</a>, a development which led to major improvements in public health in the years that followed.<sup id="cite_ref-Davies1994p510_151-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davies1994p510-151">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Whilst the Unemployed Workers' Dependants (Temporary Provisions) Act 1921 provided payments for the wives and dependent children of unemployed workers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMowat1955127_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMowat1955127-157">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> The Employment of Women, Young Persons, and Children Act 1920 prohibited the employment of children below the limit of compulsory school age in railways and transport undertakings, building and engineering construction works, factories, and mines. The legislation also prohibited the employment of children in ships at sea (except in certain circumstances, such as in respect of family members employed on the same vessel).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEByrnePadfield1980204_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEByrnePadfield1980204-158">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Lloyd_George_gcf10404.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/David_Lloyd_George_gcf10404.jpg/220px-David_Lloyd_George_gcf10404.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="348" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/David_Lloyd_George_gcf10404.jpg/330px-David_Lloyd_George_gcf10404.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/David_Lloyd_George_gcf10404.jpg/440px-David_Lloyd_George_gcf10404.jpg 2x" data-file-width="758" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of David Lloyd George by <a href="/wiki/Hal_Hurst" title="Hal Hurst">Hal Hurst</a>, 1915</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/w/index.php?title=National_Health_Insurance_Act_1920&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="National Health Insurance Act 1920 (page does not exist)">National Health Insurance Act 1920</a> increased insurance benefits, and eligibility for pensions was extended to more people. The means limit for pensions was raised by about two-thirds, immigrants and their wives were allowed to receive pensions after living in Britain for ten years, and the imprisonment and "failure to work" disqualifications for receiving pensions were abolished.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The Blind Persons Act 1920 reduced the pension age for blind people from 70 to 50.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Old age pensions were nearly doubled (from £26 5s to £47 5s a year),<sup id="cite_ref-Pugh1988p139_154-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pugh1988p139-154">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> efforts were made to help returning soldiers find employment, and the <a href="/wiki/Whitley_Councils" class="mw-redirect" title="Whitley Councils">Whitley Councils</a> of employees and employers set up.<sup id="cite_ref-Pugh1988p119_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pugh1988p119-160">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Cost">Cost</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=44" title="Edit section: Cost">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The reforming efforts of the coalition government were such that, according to the historian <a href="/wiki/Kenneth_O._Morgan" title="Kenneth O. Morgan">Kenneth O. Morgan</a>, its achievements were greater than those of the pre-war Liberal governments.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> However, the reform programme was substantially rolled back by the <a href="/wiki/Geddes_Axe" title="Geddes Axe">Geddes Axe</a>, which cut public expenditure by £76&#160;million, including substantial cuts to education,<sup id="cite_ref-Pugh1988p142143_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pugh1988p142143-162">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> and abolished the Agricultural Wages Board.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHattersley2010ch._&quot;The_Perils_of_Peace&quot;,_p._&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsisbn_9781408700976page529_529&#93;_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHattersley2010ch._&quot;The_Perils_of_Peace&quot;,_p._[httpsarchiveorgdetailsisbn_9781408700976page529_529]-163">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Ireland">Ireland</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=45" title="Edit section: Ireland">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>During Asquith's premiership, the armed insurrection by Irish republicans, known as the <a href="/wiki/Easter_Rising" title="Easter Rising">Easter Rising</a>, had taken place in <a href="/wiki/Dublin" title="Dublin">Dublin</a> during Easter Week, 1916. The government responded with harsh repression; key leaders were quickly executed. The mostly Catholic Irish nationalists then underwent a dramatic change of mood, and shifted to demand vengeance and independence.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGarry2010262–263_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGarry2010262–263-165">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1917, Lloyd George called the 1917–18 <a href="/wiki/Irish_Convention" title="Irish Convention">Irish Convention</a> in an attempt to settle the outstanding <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Ireland_Act_1914" title="Government of Ireland Act 1914">Home Rule for Ireland</a> issue; however, the upsurge in republican sympathies in Ireland following the Easter Rising coupled with Lloyd George's disastrous attempt to extend <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1918" title="Conscription Crisis of 1918">conscription to Ireland</a> in April 1918 and led to the landslide victory of <a href="/wiki/Sinn_F%C3%A9in" title="Sinn Féin">Sinn Féin</a> and the wipeout of the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Parliamentary_Party" title="Irish Parliamentary Party">Irish Parliamentary Party</a> at the December 1918 election.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> Replaced by <a href="/wiki/Sinn_F%C3%A9in" title="Sinn Féin">Sinn Féin</a> MPs, they immediately declared an <a href="/wiki/Irish_Republic" title="Irish Republic">Irish Republic</a>.
</p><p>Lloyd George presided over the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Ireland_Act_1920" title="Government of Ireland Act 1920">Government of Ireland Act 1920</a> which partitioned Ireland into <a href="/wiki/Southern_Ireland_(1921%E2%80%9322)" class="mw-redirect" title="Southern Ireland (1921–22)">Southern Ireland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland" title="Northern Ireland">Northern Ireland</a> in May 1921 during the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Irish_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglo-Irish War">Anglo-Irish War</a>. Lloyd George famously declared of the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Republican_Army_(1917%E2%80%9322)" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish Republican Army (1917–22)">Irish Republican Army</a> that "We have murder by the throat!"<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> However, he soon afterwards began negotiations with IRA leaders to recognise their authority and to end a bloody conflict. Lloyd George also invited the leader of northern Irish Unionists <a href="/wiki/James_Craig,_1st_Viscount_Craigavon" title="James Craig, 1st Viscount Craigavon">James Craig</a> to the negotiations but he refused to attend. Lloyd George wrote to Craig on 14 November 1921 "Your proposal to leave the six counties under the Northern Parliament would stereotype a frontier based neither upon natural features nor broad geographical considerations by giving it the character of an international boundary. Partition upon these lines the majority of the Irish people will never accept, nor could we conscientiously attempt to enforce it."<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> (See <a href="/wiki/The_Troubles_in_Northern_Ireland_(1920%E2%80%931922)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Troubles in Northern Ireland (1920–1922)">The Troubles in Northern Ireland (1920–1922)</a>). The <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Irish_Treaty" title="Anglo-Irish Treaty">Anglo-Irish Treaty</a> was signed in December 1921 with Irish leaders. The <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Northern_Ireland" title="Parliament of Northern Ireland">Parliament of Northern Ireland</a> exercised Article 12 of the Treaty to opt out of the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Free_State" title="Irish Free State">Irish Free State</a>. A <a href="/wiki/Irish_Boundary_Commission" title="Irish Boundary Commission">Boundary Commission</a> was established to draw the boundary between the Irish Free State and Northern Ireland. Southern Ireland, representing over a fifth of the United Kingdom's territory, seceded in 1922 to form the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Free_State" title="Irish Free State">Irish Free State</a>. (See <a href="/wiki/Partition_of_Ireland" title="Partition of Ireland">Partition of Ireland</a>).
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy_crises">Foreign policy crises</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=46" title="Edit section: Foreign policy crises">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2211-lloyd-george.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/2/27/2211-lloyd-george.jpg/220px-2211-lloyd-george.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="294" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/2/27/2211-lloyd-george.jpg/330px-2211-lloyd-george.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/2/27/2211-lloyd-george.jpg/440px-2211-lloyd-george.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1719" data-file-height="2299" /></a><figcaption>Lloyd George in 1922</figcaption></figure>
<p>A series of foreign policy crises gave Lloyd George his last opportunity to hold national and international leadership. Everything went wrong.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a> got off to a slow start and was largely ineffective. The Treaty of Versailles had set up a series of temporary organisations, composed of delegations from key powers, to ensure the successful application of the Treaty. The system worked poorly. The assembly of ambassadors was repeatedly overruled and became a nonentity. Most of the commissions were deeply divided and unable to either make decisions or convince the interested parties to carry them out. The most important commission was on Reparations, and France seized full control of it.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Raymond_Poincar%C3%A9" title="Raymond Poincaré">Raymond Poincaré</a>, president of France, was intensely anti-German, was unrelenting in his demands for huge reparations, and was repeatedly challenged by Germany. France finally invaded western Germany, and Berlin responded by imposing a runaway inflation that seriously damaged the German economy and also damaged the French economy.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> The United States, after refusing to ratify the League in 1920, almost completely disassociated itself from it. In 1921, the US set up its own international programme for world disarmament that led to the successful <a href="/wiki/Washington_Naval_Conference" title="Washington Naval Conference">Washington Naval Conference</a>, leaving only a minor role for Britain. As the reparations crisis escalated, the United States seized control of it too, with the <a href="/wiki/Dawes_Plan" title="Dawes Plan">Dawes Plan</a> of 1924 by which American banks loaned large sums to Germany, which paid reparations to the Allies, who in turn paid off their war loans to the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> In 1921, Lloyd George successfully concluded the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Soviet_Trade_Agreement" title="Anglo-Soviet Trade Agreement">Anglo-Soviet Trade Agreement</a>. Despite much effort he was unable to negotiate full diplomatic relations, as the Russians rejected all repayment of Tsarist era debts, and Conservatives in Britain grew exceedingly wary of the communist threat to European stability. Indeed, <a href="/wiki/Sir_Henry_Wilson,_1st_Baronet" title="Sir Henry Wilson, 1st Baronet">Henry Wilson</a>, the Chief of the Imperial General Staff, worried that Lloyd George had become "a traitor &amp; a Bolshevist".<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyReconstruction_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyReconstruction-173">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 276–279">&#58;&#8202;276–279&#8202;</span></sup> Lloyd George in 1922 set about to make himself master of peace in the world, especially through the <a href="/wiki/Genoa_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="Genoa Conference">Genoa Conference</a> that he expected would rival Paris of 1919 in visibility, and restore his reputation. Poincaré and the French demanded a military alliance that was far beyond what the British would accept. Germany and Russia made their own <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Rapallo_(1922)" title="Treaty of Rapallo (1922)">agreement at Rapallo</a> which wrecked the Genoa conference.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Finally, Lloyd George decided to support Greece in a war against Turkey. This led to the <a href="/wiki/Chanak_Crisis" title="Chanak Crisis">Chanak Crisis</a> when the Dominions, with the exception of Newfoundland and New Zealand, rejected the British policy and refused to support the proposed war.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_crises">Domestic crises</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=47" title="Edit section: Domestic crises">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The more conservative wing of the Unionist Party had no intention of introducing reforms, which led to three years of frustrated fighting within the coalition both between the National Liberals and the Unionists and between factions within the Conservatives themselves. Many Conservatives were angered by the granting of independence to the Irish Free State and by <a href="/wiki/Edwin_Montagu" title="Edwin Montagu">Edwin Montagu</a>'s moves towards <a href="/wiki/Montagu%E2%80%93Chelmsford_Reforms" title="Montagu–Chelmsford Reforms">limited self-government for India</a>, while a <a href="/wiki/Depression_of_1920%E2%80%9321" class="mw-redirect" title="Depression of 1920–21">sharp economic downturn</a> and <a href="/wiki/Triple_Alliance_(1914)#The_Post-War_Triple_Alliance" title="Triple Alliance (1914)">wave of strikes</a> in 1921 damaged Lloyd George's credibility. The "cash for patronage" scandal erupted in 1922 when it became known that Lloyd George had essentially sold <a href="/wiki/Peerages_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Peerages in the United Kingdom">peerages</a> (from 1917 to 1922 more than 120 hereditary peers were created) and lesser honours such as knighthoods, with a "price list for peerages" (£10,000 for a knighthood, £40,000 for a baronetcy), to raise funds for his party, via <a href="/wiki/Maundy_Gregory" title="Maundy Gregory">Maundy Gregory</a>. This was not illegal at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> A major attack in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a> on his corruption followed, resulting in the <a href="/wiki/Honours_(Prevention_of_Abuses)_Act_1925" title="Honours (Prevention of Abuses) Act 1925">Honours (Prevention of Abuses) Act 1925</a>. Other complaints were that the Cabinet contained too many Scots, too few men from Oxbridge and the great public schools, too many businessmen, and too few gentlemen.<sup id="cite_ref-CrosbyTo_Straighten_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrosbyTo_Straighten-177">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 330–331">&#58;&#8202;330–331&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Fall_from_power.2C_1922"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Fall_from_power,_1922">Fall from power, 1922</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=48" title="Edit section: Fall from power, 1922">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Lloyd_George_statue_next_to_Caernarfon_Castle.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/David_Lloyd_George_statue_next_to_Caernarfon_Castle.JPG/220px-David_Lloyd_George_statue_next_to_Caernarfon_Castle.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/David_Lloyd_George_statue_next_to_Caernarfon_Castle.JPG/330px-David_Lloyd_George_statue_next_to_Caernarfon_Castle.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/David_Lloyd_George_statue_next_to_Caernarfon_Castle.JPG/440px-David_Lloyd_George_statue_next_to_Caernarfon_Castle.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2308" data-file-height="1731" /></a><figcaption>Lloyd George statue at Caernarfon Castle (1921), in recognition of his service as local MP and prime minister</figcaption></figure>
<p>The coalition was dealt its final blow in October 1922. The Conservatives felt let down by France over the <a href="/wiki/Chanak_Crisis" title="Chanak Crisis">Chanak Crisis</a>, with Law telling France, "We cannot act alone as the policeman of the world."<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> The Conservative leader, <a href="/wiki/Austen_Chamberlain" title="Austen Chamberlain">Austen Chamberlain</a>, summoned a <a href="/wiki/Carlton_Club_meeting,_19_October_1922" class="mw-redirect" title="Carlton Club meeting, 19 October 1922">meeting of Conservative members of parliament</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Carlton_Club" title="Carlton Club">Carlton Club</a> to discuss their attitude to the Coalition in the forthcoming election. Chamberlain and most Conservative leaders supported Lloyd George; however, the rank and file rejected the coalition. The main attack came from <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin" title="Stanley Baldwin">Stanley Baldwin</a>, then President of the Board of Trade, who spoke of Lloyd George as a "dynamic force" who would break the Conservative Party. They sealed Lloyd George's fate on 19 October 1922 by voting in favour of the motion to end the coalition and fight the election "as an independent party, with its own leader and its own programme". Lloyd George submitted his resignation to the King that afternoon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamsden1998244_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamsden1998244-180">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Later_political_career_.281922.E2.80.931945.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_political_career_(1922–1945)">Later political career (1922–1945)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=49" title="Edit section: Later political career (1922–1945)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Liberal_reunion">Liberal reunion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=50" title="Edit section: Liberal reunion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Lloyd_George_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_15306.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/David_Lloyd_George_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_15306.jpg/170px-David_Lloyd_George_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_15306.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="237" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/David_Lloyd_George_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_15306.jpg/255px-David_Lloyd_George_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_15306.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/David_Lloyd_George_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_15306.jpg/340px-David_Lloyd_George_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_15306.jpg 2x" data-file-width="503" data-file-height="700" /></a><figcaption>David Lloyd George</figcaption></figure>
<p>Throughout the 1920s Lloyd George remained highly visible in politics; predictions that he would return to power were common, but it never happened.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> He still controlled a large fund (thought to have been between £1m and £3m, or £50m–£150m at 2015 prices) from his investments in newspaper ownership and from his sale of titles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKoss1985259–261_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoss1985259–261-183">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Before the <a href="/wiki/1923_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1923 United Kingdom general election">1923 election</a>, he resolved his dispute with Asquith, allowing the Liberals to run a united ticket against <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin" title="Stanley Baldwin">Stanley Baldwin</a>'s policy of protective tariffs. Baldwin both feared and despised Lloyd George, and one of his aims was to keep him out of power. He later claimed that he had adopted tariffs, which cost the Conservatives their majority, out of concern that Lloyd George was about to do so on his return from a tour of North America. Although there was press speculation at the time that Lloyd George would do so (or adopt <a href="/wiki/US-style_Prohibition" class="mw-redirect" title="US-style Prohibition">US-style Prohibition</a> to appeal to <a href="/wiki/Representation_of_the_People_Act_1918" title="Representation of the People Act 1918">newly enfranchised</a> women voters), there is no evidence that this was his intent.<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> Asquith and Lloyd George reached agreement on 13 November 1923 and issued a joint Free Trade manifesto, followed by a more general one. Lloyd George agreed to contribute £100,000 (in the event he claimed to have contributed £160,000 including help given to individual candidates; Liberal HQ put the figure at £90,000).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKoss1985261–263_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoss1985261–263-186">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1924, Lloyd George, realising that Liberal defeat was inevitable and keen to take control of the party himself, spent only £60,000.<sup id="cite_ref-RowlandGenuinely_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RowlandGenuinely-187">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 631">&#58;&#8202;631&#8202;</span></sup> At the <a href="/wiki/1924_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1924 United Kingdom general election">1924 general election</a>, Baldwin won a clear victory. Despite having a large majority, he appointed the leading coalitionists such as Austen Chamberlain and Lord Birkenhead (and former Liberal <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a>) to senior cabinet places, to discourage any restoration of the 1916–1922 coalition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECharmley1995201_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECharmley1995201-188">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Liberal_leader">Liberal leader</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=51" title="Edit section: Liberal leader">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The disastrous election result in 1924 left the Liberals as a weak third party in British politics behind the ascendant <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour Party</a>, with just over 40 MPs. Although Asquith, who had again lost his seat and was created an <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Oxford_and_Asquith" title="Earl of Oxford and Asquith">Earl</a>, remained Liberal leader, Lloyd George was elected chairman of the Liberal MPs by 26 votes to 7. <a href="/wiki/John_Simon,_1st_Viscount_Simon" title="John Simon, 1st Viscount Simon">Sir John Simon</a> and his followers were still loyal to Asquith (after 1931 Simon would lead a breakaway <a href="/wiki/National_Liberal_Party_(UK,_1931)" title="National Liberal Party (UK, 1931)">National Liberal Party</a>, which eventually merged with the Conservatives) whilst <a href="/wiki/Walter_Runciman,_1st_Viscount_Runciman_of_Doxford" title="Walter Runciman, 1st Viscount Runciman of Doxford">Walter Runciman</a> led a separate radical group within the Parliamentary Party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKoss1985272_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoss1985272-189">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lloyd George was now mainly interested in the reform of land ownership, but had only been permitted to put a brief paragraph about it in the hastily drafted 1924 Liberal manifesto. In the autumn of 1925, despite the hostility of <a href="/wiki/Charles_Hobhouse" title="Charles Hobhouse">Charles Hobhouse</a>, Runciman and <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Mond,_1st_Baron_Melchett" title="Alfred Mond, 1st Baron Melchett">Alfred Mond</a>, he began an independent campaign, soon to become "The Land and the Nation" (the <i>Green Book</i>, first of a series of policy papers produced by Lloyd George in the late 1920s). Asquith rebuked him, but was ignored; they reached an agreement in principle on 2 December, then together they presented Lloyd George's plans to the <a href="/wiki/National_Liberal_Federation" title="National Liberal Federation">National Liberal Federation</a> on 26 February 1926.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKoss1985267,_272–274_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoss1985267,_272–274-190">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJenkins1964513–514_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJenkins1964513–514-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Liberal Shadow Cabinet, including Lloyd George, unequivocally backed Baldwin's handling of the <a href="/wiki/1926_United_Kingdom_general_strike" title="1926 United Kingdom general strike">General Strike</a> on 3 May 1926, but Lloyd George then wrote an article for the American press more sympathetic to the strikers, and did not attend the Shadow Cabinet on 10 May, sending his apologies on "policy grounds". Asquith sent him a public letter (20 May) rebuking him for not attending the meeting to discuss his opinions with colleagues in private. Lloyd George's letter of 10 May had not been published, making it appear that Asquith had fired the first shot, and Lloyd George sent a public reply, moderate in tone (the journalist <a href="/wiki/C._P._Scott" title="C. P. Scott">C. P. Scott</a> helped him draft it), on 25 May. In late May, the executive of the National Liberal Federation convened to plan the agenda for the following month's conference. 16 were pro-Asquith and 8 pro-Lloyd George; they planned a motion expressing confidence in Asquith, but another option was also proposed to seek Asquith's opinion first, and also general feeling of regret at having been forced to choose between Asquith and Lloyd George. Asquith then wrote another public letter (1 June) stating that he regarded Lloyd George's behaviour as tantamount to resignation, the same as if a Cabinet Minister had refused to abide by the principle of collective responsibility. Twelve leading Liberals wrote in Asquith's support to <i>The Times</i> (1 June); however, Lloyd George had more support in the wider party than among the grandees: the London Liberal Candidates' Association (3 June) defied its officers and expressed its dismay at the split, effectively supporting Lloyd George, and on 8 June the Liberal MPs voted 20:10 urging a reconciliation. Asquith had planned to launch a fightback at the National Liberal Federation in <a href="/wiki/Weston-super-Mare" title="Weston-super-Mare">Weston-super-Mare</a>, but on 12 June, five days before the conference was due to start, he suffered a stroke which put him out of action for three months. Lloyd George was given a rapturous welcome. Asquith resigned as party leader in October 1926, dying in 1928.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKoss1985276–280_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoss1985276–280-192">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJenkins1964514–516_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJenkins1964514–516-193">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Liberal leader at last, Lloyd George used his fund to finance candidates and put forward innovative ideas for public works to reduce unemployment, detailed in works such as <i><a href="/wiki/Britain%27s_Industrial_Future" title="Britain&#39;s Industrial Future">Britain's Industrial Future</a></i> (known as the <i>Yellow Book</i>), and <i>We Can Conquer Unemployment</i> (known as the <i>Orange Book</i>). <a href="/wiki/Charles_Masterman" title="Charles Masterman">Charles Masterman</a>, a member of the commission which prepared <i>Britain's Industrial Future</i>, wrote: "When Lloyd George came back to the party, ideas came back to the party".<sup id="cite_ref-MastermanLabour_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MastermanLabour-194">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 345–346">&#58;&#8202;345–346&#8202;</span></sup> Lloyd George was helped by <a href="/wiki/John_Maynard_Keynes" title="John Maynard Keynes">John Maynard Keynes</a> to write <i>We Can Conquer Unemployment</i>, setting out economic policies to solve unemployment. In 1927, Lloyd George gave £300,000 and an annual grant of between £30,000 and £40,000 for the operations of the Liberal headquarters. He also gave £2,000 per annum to the parliamentary party until 1931.<sup id="cite_ref-JonesInOppo_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JonesInOppo-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 223–224">&#58;&#8202;223–224&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-RowlandGenuinely_187-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RowlandGenuinely-187">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 630">&#58;&#8202;630&#8202;</span></sup> Even with the money, the results at the <a href="/wiki/1929_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1929 United Kingdom general election">1929 general election</a> were disappointing. The Liberals increased their support only to 60 or so seats, while Labour became the largest party for the first time. Once again, the Liberals ended up supporting a minority Labour government. In 1929, Lloyd George became <a href="/wiki/Father_of_the_House_(United_Kingdom)" title="Father of the House (United Kingdom)">Father of the House</a> (longest-serving member of the Commons), an honorific position without power.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Marginalised">Marginalised</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=52" title="Edit section: Marginalised">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vera_%26_Chaim_Weizmann,_Herbert_Samuel,_Lloyd_George,_Ethel_Snowden,_Philip_Snowden.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Vera_%26_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Herbert_Samuel%2C_Lloyd_George%2C_Ethel_Snowden%2C_Philip_Snowden.jpeg/230px-Vera_%26_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Herbert_Samuel%2C_Lloyd_George%2C_Ethel_Snowden%2C_Philip_Snowden.jpeg" decoding="async" width="230" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Vera_%26_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Herbert_Samuel%2C_Lloyd_George%2C_Ethel_Snowden%2C_Philip_Snowden.jpeg/345px-Vera_%26_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Herbert_Samuel%2C_Lloyd_George%2C_Ethel_Snowden%2C_Philip_Snowden.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Vera_%26_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Herbert_Samuel%2C_Lloyd_George%2C_Ethel_Snowden%2C_Philip_Snowden.jpeg/460px-Vera_%26_Chaim_Weizmann%2C_Herbert_Samuel%2C_Lloyd_George%2C_Ethel_Snowden%2C_Philip_Snowden.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="2399" data-file-height="1746" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Vera_Weizmann" title="Vera Weizmann">Vera Weizmann</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chaim_Weizmann" title="Chaim Weizmann">Chaim Weizmann</a>, <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Samuel" class="mw-redirect" title="Herbert Samuel">Herbert Samuel</a>, Lloyd George, <a href="/wiki/Ethel_Snowden" title="Ethel Snowden">Ethel Snowden</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Philip_Snowden" class="mw-redirect" title="Philip Snowden">Philip Snowden</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1931, an illness prevented Lloyd George's joining the <a href="/wiki/UK_National_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="UK National Government">National Government</a> when it was formed. When the National Government later called a general election he tried to pull the Liberal Party out of it, but succeeded in taking only a few followers, most of whom were related to him; the main Liberal Party remained in the coalition for a year longer, under the leadership of Sir <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Samuel,_1st_Viscount_Samuel" title="Herbert Samuel, 1st Viscount Samuel">Herbert Samuel</a>. By the 1930s Lloyd George was on the margins of British politics, although still intermittently in the public eye and publishing his <i>War Memoirs</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-196">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Lloyd_George.27s_.22New_Deal.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Lloyd_George's_&quot;New_Deal&quot;">Lloyd George's "New Deal"</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=53" title="Edit section: Lloyd George&#039;s &quot;New Deal&quot;">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:LloydGeorgeEn1932.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/LloydGeorgeEn1932.jpeg/220px-LloydGeorgeEn1932.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/LloydGeorgeEn1932.jpeg/330px-LloydGeorgeEn1932.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/LloydGeorgeEn1932.jpeg/440px-LloydGeorgeEn1932.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="679" data-file-height="542" /></a><figcaption>Lloyd George in 1932</figcaption></figure>
<p>In January 1935 Lloyd George announced a programme of economic reform, called "Lloyd George's New Deal" after the American <a href="/wiki/New_Deal" title="New Deal">New Deal</a>. This <a href="/wiki/John_Maynard_Keynes" title="John Maynard Keynes">Keynesian</a> economic programme was essentially the same as that of 1929. MacDonald requested that he put his case before the Cabinet. In March, Lloyd George submitted a 100-page memorandum (published as <i>Organizing Prosperity: A Scheme of National Reconstruction</i>)<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> that was cross-examined between April and June in ten meetings of the Cabinet's sub-committee; however, the programme did not find favour; two-thirds of Conservative MPs were against Lloyd George joining the National government, and some Cabinet members would have resigned if he had joined.<sup id="cite_ref-JonesInOppo_195-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JonesInOppo-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 238–239">&#58;&#8202;238–239&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Support_for_Nazi_Germany">Support for Nazi Germany</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=54" title="Edit section: Support for Nazi Germany">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Lloyd George was consistently pro-German after 1923,<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> in part due to his growing conviction that Germany had been treated unfairly at Versailles. He supported German demands for territorial concessions and recognition of its "great power" status; he paid much less attention to the security concerns of France, Poland, Czechoslovakia, and Belgium.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERudman2011ch._5–8_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERudman2011ch._5–8-199">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> In a speech in 1933, he warned that if <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a> were overthrown, communism would replace him in Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> In August 1934, he insisted Germany could not wage war and assured European nations that there would be no risk of war during the next ten years.<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> In September 1936, he visited Germany to talk with Hitler. Hitler said he was pleased to have met "the man who won the war"; Lloyd George was moved, and called Hitler "the greatest living German".<sup id="cite_ref-JonesInOppo_195-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JonesInOppo-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 247">&#58;&#8202;247&#8202;</span></sup> Lloyd George also visited Germany's public works programmes and was impressed. On his return to Britain, he wrote an article for the <i><a href="/wiki/Daily_Express" title="Daily Express">Daily Express</a></i> praising Hitler and stating: "The Germans have definitely made up their minds never to quarrel with us again."<sup id="cite_ref-JonesInOppo_195-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JonesInOppo-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 248">&#58;&#8202;248&#8202;</span></sup> He believed Hitler was "the George Washington of Germany"; that he was rearming Germany for defence and not for offensive war; that a war between Germany and the Soviet Union would not happen for at least ten years; that Hitler admired the British and wanted their friendship but that there was no British leadership to exploit this; however, by 1937, Lloyd George's distaste for <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a> led him to disavow Chamberlain's appeasement policies.<sup id="cite_ref-JonesInOppo_195-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JonesInOppo-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 248">&#58;&#8202;248&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Maintains_Welsh_connection">Maintains Welsh connection</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=55" title="Edit section: Maintains Welsh connection">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Lloyd George was President of the London Welsh Trust (which runs the <a href="/wiki/London_Welsh_Centre" title="London Welsh Centre">London Welsh Centre</a>, Gray's Inn Road) from 1934 until 1935.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_196-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-196">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1936, whilst on holiday in Jamaica, Lloyd George expressed anger in a letter to his daughter Megan about the case of <a href="/wiki/Saunders_Lewis" title="Saunders Lewis">Saunders Lewis</a> being tried in England after he set fire to the RAF bombing school in Llyn. Lloyd George says that the government "tried Wales at the Old Bailey" and that "trials were never taken out of Ireland into the English courts".<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lloyd George continued to preside over the <a href="/wiki/National_Eisteddfod_of_Wales" title="National Eisteddfod of Wales">National Eisteddfod</a> at its Thursday session each summer. In September 1944 he and Frances left his home, <a href="/wiki/Bron-y-de" title="Bron-y-de">Bron-y-de</a> in <a href="/wiki/Churt" title="Churt">Churt</a>, for <a href="/wiki/T%C5%B7_Newydd" title="Tŷ Newydd">Tŷ Newydd</a>, a farm near his boyhood home in Llanystumdwy. He continued to be a MP in Caernarfon until his death in 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-Rintala1985_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rintala1985-204">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years">Final years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=56" title="Edit section: Final years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the last important parliamentary intervention of his career, which occurred during the crucial <a href="/wiki/Norway_Debate" title="Norway Debate">Norway Debate</a> of May 1940, Lloyd George made a powerful speech that helped to undermine Chamberlain as prime minister and to pave the way for the ascendancy of Churchill.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJenkins2001580_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJenkins2001580-205">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> Churchill offered Lloyd George the agriculture portfolio in his Cabinet, initially subject to Chamberlain's approval, but this condition and, once Chamberlain had withdrawn his opposition, Lloyd George's unwillingness to sit alongside Chamberlain, led him to refuse.<sup id="cite_ref-JonesInOppo_195-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JonesInOppo-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 255">&#58;&#8202;255&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> Lloyd George also thought that Britain's chances in the war were dim, and he remarked to his secretary: "I shall wait until Winston is bust."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESylvester1975281_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESylvester1975281-207">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> He wrote to the <a href="/wiki/Hastings_Russell,_12th_Duke_of_Bedford" title="Hastings Russell, 12th Duke of Bedford">Duke of Bedford</a> in September 1940, during the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Britain" title="Battle of Britain">Battle of Britain</a>, advocating a negotiated peace with Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReynolds200679_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReynolds200679-208">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Grave_of_David_Lloyd_George,_Llanystumdwy,_Gwynedd_(geograph_6447819).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Grave_of_David_Lloyd_George%2C_Llanystumdwy%2C_Gwynedd_%28geograph_6447819%29.jpg/200px-Grave_of_David_Lloyd_George%2C_Llanystumdwy%2C_Gwynedd_%28geograph_6447819%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="269" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Grave_of_David_Lloyd_George%2C_Llanystumdwy%2C_Gwynedd_%28geograph_6447819%29.jpg/300px-Grave_of_David_Lloyd_George%2C_Llanystumdwy%2C_Gwynedd_%28geograph_6447819%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Grave_of_David_Lloyd_George%2C_Llanystumdwy%2C_Gwynedd_%28geograph_6447819%29.jpg/400px-Grave_of_David_Lloyd_George%2C_Llanystumdwy%2C_Gwynedd_%28geograph_6447819%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1189" data-file-height="1600" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Grave_of_David_Lloyd_George" title="Grave of David Lloyd George">Grave of David Lloyd George</a>, Llanystumdwy</figcaption></figure>
<p>A pessimistic speech by Lloyd George on 7 May 1941 led Churchill to compare him with <a href="/wiki/Philippe_P%C3%A9tain" title="Philippe Pétain">Philippe Pétain</a>. On 11 June 1942, he made his last speech in the House of Commons, and he cast his last vote in the Commons on 18 February 1943 as one of the 121 MPs (97 Labour) condemning the Government for its failure to back the <a href="/wiki/Beveridge_Report" title="Beveridge Report">Beveridge Report</a>. Fittingly, his final vote was in defence of the welfare state which he had helped to create.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAddison1994224–225_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAddison1994224–225-209">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He continued to attend <a href="/wiki/Welsh_Church_of_Central_London" title="Welsh Church of Central London">Castle Street Baptist Chapel</a> in London, but by 1944 he was weakening rapidly and his voice failing. He was still an MP but, concerned about his health (he felt physically unable to campaign) and the wartime social changes in the constituency, he feared Carnarvon Boroughs might go Conservative at the next election.<sup id="cite_ref-Rintala1985_204-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rintala1985-204">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wishing, as the last surviving author of the Versailles settlement, to have an official platform by which to speak on any peace settlement, he accepted a peerage.<sup id="cite_ref-Rintala1985_204-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rintala1985-204">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> It was announced in the <a href="/wiki/1945_New_Year_Honours" title="1945 New Year Honours">1945 New Year Honours</a> that Lloyd George would be made an earl, which he was as <a href="/wiki/Earl_Lloyd-George_of_Dwyfor" title="Earl Lloyd-George of Dwyfor">Earl Lloyd-George of Dwyfor</a>, and Viscount Gwynedd, of Dwyfor in the <a href="/wiki/Historic_counties_of_Wales" title="Historic counties of Wales">County</a> of <a href="/wiki/Caernarvonshire" class="mw-redirect" title="Caernarvonshire">Caernarvonshire</a> on 12 February 1945; however, he did not live long enough to take his seat in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Wrigley1992_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wrigley1992-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=57" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Lloyd George died of cancer at the age of 82 on 26 March 1945, with his wife Frances and his daughter Megan at his bedside. Four days later, on <a href="/wiki/Good_Friday" title="Good Friday">Good Friday</a>, he was buried beside the <a href="/wiki/Afon_Dwyfor" title="Afon Dwyfor">river Dwyfor</a> in <a href="/wiki/Llanystumdwy" title="Llanystumdwy">Llanystumdwy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> A boulder marks the grave; there is no inscription; however, a <a href="/wiki/Grave_of_David_Lloyd_George" title="Grave of David Lloyd George">monument</a> designed by the architect Sir <a href="/wiki/Clough_Williams-Ellis" title="Clough Williams-Ellis">Clough Williams-Ellis</a> was subsequently erected around the grave,<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> bearing an <a href="/wiki/Englyn" title="Englyn">englyn</a> (strict-metre stanza) engraved on slate in his memory composed by his nephew <a href="/wiki/W._R._P._George" title="W. R. P. George">W. R. P. George</a>. Nearby stands the <a href="/wiki/Lloyd_George_Museum" title="Lloyd George Museum">Lloyd George Museum</a>, also designed by Williams-Ellis and opened in 1963.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Assessment">Assessment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=58" title="Edit section: Assessment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Lloyd George has often been <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of the United Kingdom">ranked highly among modern British prime ministers</a>, but his legacy remains complicated and controversial. Scholars have praised his welfare reforms and his efforts to mobilise and lead Britain to victory during the First World War, but he has also been criticised for adopting a "<a href="/wiki/Presidential_system" title="Presidential system">presidential</a>" style of leadership, for distrusting his own commanders during the war, and for his strategic failures and involvement in various scandals. His legacies over Ireland and the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles" title="Treaty of Versailles">Treaty of Versailles</a> are also controversial, and although an ardent <a href="/wiki/Zionist" class="mw-redirect" title="Zionist">Zionist</a>, he expressed <a href="/wiki/Antisemitic" class="mw-redirect" title="Antisemitic">antisemitic</a> views.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> In the post-war period he arguably alienated many of the workers he had earlier championed, helping to swell Labour's popular support at the Liberals' expense (not helped by his conflicts with Asquithian Liberals after 1916).
</p><p>
Historian <a href="/wiki/Martin_Pugh_(author)" class="mw-redirect" title="Martin Pugh (author)">Martin Pugh</a> in <i>The Oxford Companion to British History</i> argues that:</p><blockquote><p>[Lloyd George] made a greater impact on British public life than any other 20th-cent. statesman. He laid the foundations of what later became the welfare state, and put a progressive income tax system at the centre of government finance. He also left his mark on the system of government by enlarging the scope of the prime minister's role. He was acclaimed, not without reason, as the 'Man Who Won the War'.&#160;... he was blamed by many Liberals for destroying their party in 1918, hated in the Labour movement for his handling of industrial issues after 1918, and disparaged by Conservatives for his radicalism.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin_Pugh_2002_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin_Pugh_2002-213">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p><a href="/wiki/George_Riddell,_1st_Baron_Riddell" title="George Riddell, 1st Baron Riddell">George Riddell, 1st Baron Riddell</a>, a wealthy newspaper publisher, was a close confidant and financial supporter of Lloyd George from 1908 to 1922.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGilbert1992Preface,_p._14_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilbert1992Preface,_p._14-214">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> During Lloyd George's first year as prime minister, in summer 1917, Riddell assessed his personality in his diary:</p><blockquote><p>His energy, capacity for work, and power of recuperation are remarkable. He has an extraordinary memory, imagination, and the art of getting at the root of a matter.&#160;... He is not afraid of responsibility, and has no respect for tradition or convention. He is always ready to examine, scrap or revise established theories and practices. These qualities give him unlimited confidence in himself.&#160;... He is one of the craftiest of men, and his extraordinary charm of manner not only wins him friends, but does much to soften the asperities of his opponents and enemies. He is full of humour and a born actor.&#160;... He has an instinctive power of divining the thoughts and intentions of people with whom he is conversing&#160;... His chief defects are: (1) Lack of appreciation of existing institutions, organisations, and stolid, dull people&#160;... their ways are not his ways and their methods are not his methods. (2) Fondness for a grandiose scheme in preference to an attempt to improve existing machinery. (3) Disregard of difficulties in carrying out big projects&#160;... he is not a man of detail.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrigg2003ch._&quot;Lloyd_George&#39;s_Boswell&quot;,_pp._220–221_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrigg2003ch._&quot;Lloyd_George&#39;s_Boswell&quot;,_pp._220–221-215">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>
In 2007, historian John Shepherd wrote in <i><a href="/wiki/History_Today" title="History Today">History Today</a></i>:</p><blockquote>
<p>In any poll of modern historians Winston Churchill and David Lloyd George would emerge as the two most renowned prime ministers during the past century.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family">Family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=59" title="Edit section: Family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Margaret_and_children">Margaret and children</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_Lloyd_George&amp;action=edit&amp;section=60" title="Edit section: Margaret and children">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TNA_COPY1-560-129_David_and_Megan_Lloyd_George.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/TNA_COPY1-560-129_David_and_Megan_Lloyd_George.jpg/220px-TNA_COPY1-560-129_David_and_Megan_Lloyd_George.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="340" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/TNA_COPY1-560-129_David_and_Megan_Lloyd_George.jpg/330px-TNA_COPY1-560-129_David_and_Megan_Lloyd_George.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/TNA_COPY1-560-129_David_and_Megan_Lloyd_George.jpg/440px-TNA_COPY1-560-129_David_and_Megan_Lloyd_George.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="926" /></a><figcaption>Lloyd George with his daughter Megan in 1911</figcaption></figure>
<p>He had five children by his first wife, <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Lloyd_George" title="Margaret Lloyd George">Margaret</a>:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Richard_Lloyd_George,_2nd_Earl_Lloyd-George_of_Dwyfor" title="Richard Lloyd George, 2nd Earl Lloyd-George of Dwyfor">Richard Lloyd George, 2nd Earl Lloyd-George of Dwyfor</a> (1889–1968), army officer</li>
<li>Mair Eluned Lloyd George (1890–1907, who died during an <a href="/wiki/Appendectomy" title="Appendectomy">appendectomy</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Olwen_Carey_Evans" title="Olwen Carey Evans">Olwen Elizabeth Lloyd George</a> (1892–1990), humanitarian and writer</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Gwilym_Lloyd_George" title="Gwilym Lloyd George">Gwilym Lloyd George, 1st Viscount Tenby</a> (1894–1967), <a href="/wiki/National_Liberal_Party_(UK,_1931)" title="National Liberal Party (UK, 1931)">National Liberal</a> politician</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Megan_Lloyd_George" title="Megan Lloyd George">Megan Arvon Lloyd George</a> (1902–1966), <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour</a> politician</li></ul>
<p>Despite his long-term affair with Frances Stevenson, he remained married to Margaret, and remained fond of her until her death<sup id="cite_ref-LongfordGreater_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LongfordGreater-217">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 6">&#58;&#8202;6&#8202;</span></sup> on 20 January 1941; Lloyd George was deeply upset by the fact that bad weather prevented him from being with her when she died.
</p><p>Gwilym and Megan both followed their father into politics and were elected Members of Parliament.
